#10552. How to Reduce Hallucinations in LLMs

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Ask one of the newest language models whether a backpack comes in green, and it'll usually admit it doesn't know. Ask it how to return that backpack, and it may send the customer to a returns page, an account screen, and a return label that the store has never had. That's still a hallucination: an answer that sounds right, comes with total confidence, and isn't true.

Whatever you're building, the fix is the same: stop trusting the model with the parts it can get wrong, and check its work before anyone sees it.

In this tutorial we'll build a small support bot for a made-up outdoor gear store, three times over. The first version is the prompt plenty of bots start with. Then we'll apply five fixes, run six tricky customer emails through every version, and finish with a bot where the model can't slip a made-up price, date, or product past the code.

An LLM hallucination is output that's fluent and confident but not backed by the facts: a product that doesn't exist, a policy nobody wrote, a date that's a week out, or a citation to a paper that was never published. The word covers two different failures:

Each one needs a different fix, so it helps to keep them apart.

A language model writes by predicting likely text, one token at a time. It has no separate store of facts it checks against, and nothing in that process says "stop, you don't know this". Training adds to the problem. OpenAI's research on why language models hallucinate points out that when models are graded only on how many answers they get right, a guess scores better than "I don't know", so they learn to guess.

Newer models are much better at the first one. They'll often say they don't know a price rather than invent one. But they still fill gaps with what's typical, and what's typical isn't always what's true for your business. You can't train that out of a model you call through an API. What you can change is what you ask it to do, what you give it, and what you check.

A made-up answer costs more than one bad reply. Some of the costs are already on the record:

In a support inbox, the cost is quieter but adds up: a refund you didn't owe, or a customer who goes looking for a live chat button that isn't there and doesn't come back.

Northwind Outdoor sells four things: two packs, a rain cover, and a tent. It has two orders on file and three short policies. Here's all of it:

Six emails come in, and each one is there to trip the bot in a different way:

The traps: the 40L pack doesn't come in green, but the 28L does. The 40L is water-resistant, not waterproof. There's no 60L. Whether the return is allowed depends on a membership, a sale price, and a count of days since delivery. The tracking details are in one order record. And the store has no price match policy at all.

Make a folder, add "type": "module" to its package.json, and install the Anthropic SDK and Zod:

Every version calls the model through one small helper:

We're using Claude Opus 5.5 at low effort. These are short, simple replies, so the model doesn't need to think for long. Swap in any model you like, because none of the fixes depend on it.

Opus 5.5 didn't invent a single price, colour, or delivery date across the six emails. Every reply said it couldn't see the store's details. That's real progress compared with older models, but look at what it said instead. Here's the reply to the email about a pack the store doesn't sell:

It treats the Ridgeline 60L as real and offers to help pick a fit. The other replies made up the store around the customer:

Northwind has none of those. None of it is a wild guess: it's what a typical online store has. That's exactly why it's dangerous. A customer who goes looking for the live chat or the newsletter discount won't find it, and the bot never answered the question anyway.

The model can't know Northwind's prices, so give them to it. This is the idea behind retrieval-augmented generation (RAG): find the records that matter and put them in the prompt. Our store is small enough to send all of it.

Two things changed. The store data goes in the system prompt, wrapped in tags so the model can tell data from instructions. And the prompt tells it to answer only from that data. Here's the green pack email again:

Right on all three counts, and it even pointed to the pack that does come in green. The return, which needs four facts from two places, came back right as well:

Delivered October 6, a Trail Club member, so 60 days, so December 5. Bought on sale, so store credit for what was paid, not the list price. Version 2 got all six emails right.

The last line of that prompt matters as much as the data: "If the answer isn't in the data, say you don't know and that a team member will reply by email." Without it, a model that can't find the answer has to choose between letting the customer down and guessing, and training has taught it to guess. With it, "I don't know" is a right answer, not a failure.

The price match email is the test, since the store data says nothing about price matching:

It said it didn't know, passed the question to a person, and answered the parts it could from the data.

If version 2 got everything right, why build a version 3? Because "right on this run" isn't the same as "right every time":

The next three fixes take the facts and the rules away from the model completely.

Version 3 splits the work into three steps. First, the model reads the email, but instead of writing a reply, it fills in a form. Every field is a choice from a list, a yes or no, or text copied from the email:

Zod describes the form, and z.toJSONSchema() turns it into a JSON schema for the API's structured outputs. The API holds the model to that schema, so product can only be one of our four product IDs, not_sold, or none. There's no way for it to answer ridgeline-60. Zod then checks the answer again, so the rest of the code gets a typed object. SitePoint's guide to LLM structured output with JSON Schema goes deeper on this pattern.

The model is still reading natural language, which it's very good at. It just isn't trusted to know what the store sells anymore.

A form also makes the model's mistakes easy to see. The first time I ran this, the form had no asks_something_else field, and the model filed the price match email under product. The reply didn't make anything up, but the email never reached a person. In version 2, a mistake like that would be hidden inside a paragraph. Here it was one wrong value in a JSON object, and the fix was one more field.

If all you need from this step is decisions like these, a model built for typed answers can do it faster and for less. Our typed AI pipeline tutorial builds the same kind of email sorter with Jev and Clairic.

Second step: plain code takes the form, looks up the product and the order, applies the rules, and does the maths. What comes out is a list of facts in plain sentences:

Every number in there was worked out by code that does the same thing every time, and that you can unit test. The 34 days, the December 5 deadline, and the $151.20 in store credit come out the same on every run, whatever the model does.

The last rule in decide() matters most. If an email asks something the rules don't cover, the facts say so and the email goes to a person. Code decides when to say "I don't know", not the model.

Third step: the model writes the reply from the facts and nothing else. Writing is the one job here it does better than code.

Even with the right facts, a model writing a reply can still add something: a helpful "ships in 1–2 business days", or a price it remembers from somewhere. So the last step checks the reply. Anything that looks like a number, an order number, or a product name has to appear in the email or the facts:

It's simple on purpose. It doesn't understand the reply. It just refuses to let through a value that came from nowhere, and that covers the hallucinations that cost money: prices, dates, quantities, and products. Nothing in the reply can talk it out of a result.

The writer runs the check on every draft:

If a draft fails, the model gets one retry and is told exactly which values weren't in the facts. If the retry fails too, the customer gets the facts as a plain list. That's less charming, but it's true.

A check like this will raise false alarms, and that's fine. On my first run, it read a line about the tent's 2.1 kg weight as a product called "Weighs 2" and sent the draft back for a retry. The weight was right, and it was the only thing flagged. A false alarm costs a few seconds. A missed one costs a customer. I tightened the patterns (the version above has the fix), but I'd still rather it flag too much than too little.

It's the same thing that separates the better agentic AI coding tools from autocomplete: they run the tests and read the errors instead of trusting their first draft. Model output isn't finished until something other than the model has checked it.

This script sends each email through all three versions, saves every reply, and runs the check from Fix 5 over all of them. Versions 1 and 2 are checked against everything the store knows:

The check caught one thing in version 1: the "1–2 business days" it made up for processing an order. It missed the "up to 24 hours" for tracking in the same reply, but only because $24 is the rain cover's price, so 24 counted as a known number. And it couldn't catch the invented returns page or live chat at all, because they aren't numbers. That's why the check is the last fix, not the only one.

Reading all 18 replies, here's how each version did:

Version 3 was less helpful than version 2 in one place. It couldn't give the rain cover's price, because decide() only adds facts about the product the email names. Version 2 had the whole store to look at. In version 3, the fix is a rule you write once: when a product has an add-on, include the add-on's facts too.

Here's version 3's reply to the price match email:

No model is free of hallucinations, so you can't stop one from ever making something up. What you can do is build so a made-up detail can't reach a customer: let code own the facts, and check the model's output against them before it's used. Where it matters, send anything your rules don't cover to a person.

It cuts them a lot, because the model no longer has to guess facts it was never given. In our test, putting the store's data in the prompt took the bot from no useful answers to six right ones. It doesn't stop the model misreading what it retrieved, mixing up two records, or filling a gap the retrieved text leaves. Pair it with a way out and a check on the output.

Not really. Temperature controls how random the wording is. A low temperature makes the model give the same answer more often, including the same wrong answer.

Collect inputs that have tripped your app (or are likely to), write down the right answer for each, and run every version of your prompt or pipeline over the same set. Automated checks like the one in this tutorial catch made-up values. Read the replies yourself for the rest, because the made-up returns page above would pass any number check.

Hallucinations come from asking a model to do jobs it isn't built for: knowing facts it was never given, and running small programs in its head. The five fixes take those jobs away:

The model still does what it's best at: reading messy text and writing a friendly reply. It just isn't trusted with the facts anymore. From here, try a seventh email that asks two things at once, add the add-on rule to decide(), or extend the check to catch colours and sizes as well as numbers.

#10551. Samsung bets that you’ll either buy a Galaxy S27 Pro or Ultra next year

Samsung is teeing up to kick off 2027 with an all-new lineup of flagship smartphones, and despite rising prices at your local Best Buy, the brand is still hedging that you’re going to pony up for the most premium model available.

As reported by ETNews, Samsung is currently allotting over 70 percent of its total production capabilities to just the Galaxy S27 Ultra and the new Galaxy S27 “Pro” model expected to launch alongside it. That leaves the plain Galaxy S27 and the larger S27+ on the outside looking in. In comparison to this year’s launch, the Galaxy S26 Ultra made up about 50 percent of this series’ production volume, for a grand total of 18 million units.

Historical performance implies Samsung is probably correct here. While big displays are typically what consumers are drawn to, it does appear that the S27 Pro won’t be all that much smaller than the S27+. Considering the Pro should slot in right around the typical price point as the Plus model, it’s not hard to imagine plenty of would-be S27+ buyers moving to the Pro.

For what it’s worth, Samsung betting big on high-end devices usually pays off. We’re on year two of the Fold series seeing big successes — first with the redesigned Fold 7 in 2025, then with the reshaped Fold 8 this year — and the Galaxy S26 Ultra managed to become the best-selling Android phone in Q2 of this year in a list typically populated by iPhones and cheap Galaxy A-series models. Even with prices constantly on the rise, the people yearn for flagship smartphones. You just have to wonder where that ceiling actually is.

#10550. Mass smuggling of up to 1.5 million Nvidia AI GPUs could be the underlying cause of Jensen Huang's departure, analysts claim

Hardware smuggling to China could be the reason why Jensen had to leave the company he co-founded.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Leading semiconductor research firm SemiAnalysis believes that Jensen Huang's sudden departure from Nvidia may have been driven not only by revelations about his personal extramarital relationships, but also by widespread illicit exports of advanced AI accelerators to China. In its latest newsletter, analysts estimate that more than 1.5 gigawatts (GW) worth of restricted Nvidia hardware, which could be equivalent to up to 1.5 million GPUs, has reached the country despite U.S. export controls.

Over 1.5 GW of Nvidia hardware subject to U.S. export restrictions ultimately ended up in China in recent years, according to SemiAnalysis' Accelerator Model, which tracks AI accelerator production and destinations. The estimate excludes processors that Nvidia specifically developed to comply with U.S. export rules, such as the H20 or H800. The firm also claims that the U.S. Department of Commerce has reached a similar conclusion about the scale of unauthorized shipments. Yet, SemiAnalysis does not disclose the exact number of accelerators shipped to China illegally, or their split between Nvidia's product generations.

The scale of the alleged diversions is particularly notable because modern AI accelerators require substantial power and infrastructure. While the report does not explain how it converts accelerator quantities into power figures, it is likely that the company uses package-level nameplate/rated power multiplied by the number of GPUs. Assuming that all of these AI GPUs were Nvidia H200 SXM rated for up to 700W, then China got around 2.142 million of such accelerators, while if all of these processors were B200 SXM rated for up to 1,000W, then we are talking about 1.5 million units. For a mixed deployment, the total would fall between these figures, depending on the proportion of H200 and B200 accelerators. To put these numbers into context, xAI's Colossus One had 220-230,000 Hopper H100 and H200 GPUs, whereas the Colossus 2 has a target of 1.2 million Blackwell B200 and B300 GPUs. That said, 1.5 GW of restricted Nvidia hardware in China represents an enormous amount of computing infrastructure, which is comparable, or even exceeds, the scale of xAI's entire existing Colossus operation.

While SemiAnalysis did not reveal all the cases in which restricted AI accelerators reached the People's Republic of China, it supports its assessment with two well-known cases of alleged GPU smuggling. The first involves Supermicro co-founder Wally Liaw, Taiwan sales executive Steven Chang, and contractor Willy Sun, who were charged in March 2026. According to the indictment, approximately $2.5 billion worth of servers were sold to a company in Southeast Asia, with $510 million worth of equipment subsequently diverted to China and obtained by Alibaba. Prosecutors allege that the defendants used substitute servers and transferred identification labels to conceal the actual destinations of the hardware during audits.

The second case involves nine individuals associated with Nvidia, Albatron, Chief Telecom, Flying Tiger, and Supermicro in Taiwan. The authorities accuse the group of arranging unauthorized exports of 130 Nvidia B300-based servers, 74 of which eventually reached China. The suspects used shell companies, forged documents, and declared Japan as the destination for servers that were actually bound for China, according to prosecutors. Taiwanese authorities discovered the operation during a drug smuggling investigation. Both cases are still before the courts.

Amidst the ongoing fallout and reports into Jensen's Huang's extramarital affair with Alice Huang, an alleged Chinese intelligence asset who was an executive at Megaspeed, one report claims that Ms. Huang was personally responsible for facilitating the smuggling of $7.5 billion of Nvidia AI hardware into China, around 240,000 GPUs, mostly H100 and H200 models.

SemiAnalysis suggests that the scale of these violations may explain why Huang's personal conduct became a major issue at this very point in time. The company's analysts speculate that some members of the U.S. intelligence community sought to remove Jensen Huang because he opposed more aggressive restrictions on China's access to American AI hardware. However, the report provides no direct evidence that intelligence agencies influenced Nvidia's board or that Huang participated in unauthorized exports.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

The timing also coincides with congressional efforts to advance the Remote Access Security Act (RASA), which passed the House of Representatives on January 12. The legislation is meant to extend U.S. export-control authority to remote access to advanced AI hardware hosted outside China, including data centers in Europe and the Middle East. SemiAnalysis reports that Nvidia and Oracle have actively opposed the proposal.

The analysts believe that Huang's departure essentially removes an influential opponent of stricter restrictions and accelerates efforts to block Chinese companies from accessing American AI hardware in the cloud. However, SemiAnalysis warns that truly all-inclusive restrictions could encourage Chinese developers to adopt domestic accelerators (something that Jensen Huang has consistently warned about), undermine international AI software collaboration, and ultimately weaken American high-tech companies.

Continued Chinese access to American AI accelerators offers several advantages to the U.S. technology industry, according to SemiAnalysis. Firstly, it generates revenue for American companies such as AMD, Nvidia, and cloud service providers. Secondly, when Chinese companies rent GPU capacity from data centers outside China, U.S. authorities retain the ability to monitor their activities and potentially terminate access if necessary due to national security concerns. Thirdly, prolonged reliance on American hardware reduces incentives for Chinese developers to adopt domestic AI accelerators, which could eventually compete with AMD and Nvidia in international markets.

Another important aspect is the contribution of Chinese developers to the global software ecosystem. On the one hand, Chinese laboratories have developed open-source technologies such as DeepEP, DeepGEMM, and MSA, which improve the efficiency of AI workloads on Nvidia hardware and benefit developers worldwide. In addition, American companies also depend on Chinese engineers, component suppliers, and manufacturers. Therefore, legal curbs that completely restrict Chinese developers' access to American AI accelerators could undermine international cooperation, reduce availability of open-source optimizations, and encourage Chinese companies to develop software specifically for domestic hardware.

On the other hand, access to advanced AI accelerators from AMD or Nvidia enables Chinese laboratories to develop increasingly sophisticated AI models, which will rival leading-edge models developed in America and perhaps create national security risks. To that end, SemiAnalysis acknowledges the need to limit China's access to advanced AI accelerators from U.S.-based companies, as they could, among other things, be used to develop weapons of mass destruction or undermine American national security in different ways. Yet, SA analysts argue that restrictions should focus on the amount and capabilities of hardware available to Chinese entities rather than completely prohibit any access.

SemiAnalysis seems to favor a system in which Chinese developers can rent computing capacity from trusted providers outside China under U.S. oversight. Such an approach could preserve American technological influence and commercial benefits and limit China's ability to acquire advanced AI infrastructure independently.

#10548. Humble Bundle redesign removes two-factor verification from settings

A new look for the Humble Bundle storefront is missing an important security feature that was previously there.

A new look for the Humble Bundle storefront is missing an important security feature that was previously there.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

The Humble Bundle storefront for buying games and other software recently got a new look, but on the settings page, it no longer offers a way to add or update two-factor verification methods, as reported by Kotaku. PC Gamer also points out that there are Humble Bundle support pages discussing two-factor verification, and the writer for the publication said that they had set up two-factor verification on the old version of the site.

However, it does appear that, at least as of now, there is some form of two-factor verification. When I tried logging into my Humble Bundle account while writing this article, I was prompted to check my email for an access code before I could access my account. While logged in, I was able to update my password, but I didn’t have to enter my old password to be able to do so.

For what it’s worth, my passwords app also shows that I set up a rotating two-factor code for Humble Bundle at some point. I was not asked to input that at any part of my login process today.

Humble Bundle owner IGN Entertainment and IGN parent company Ziff Davis didn’t immediately reply to a request for comment.

Save on this CyberPower PC with Intel Core Ultra 9 285, RTX 5070 Ti, 32GB DDR5, and a 2TB SSD.

The main event in this build is the potent GPU. With the coveted 16GB of VRAM capacity and 8,960 CUDA cores, the RTX 5070 Ti excels at gaming in 1080p and 1440p, and with access to Nvidia's DLSS technologies, will also be ample for some 4K gaming too.

The Intel Core Ultra 9 285 is a 24-core CPU with eight performance cores and 16 efficient cores, with a total of 24 threads. It has max turbo clocks of 5.6 GHz, and while it lacks the potent cache of AMD's X3D SKUs, it is a brilliant CPU for multi-threaded workloads.

This PC also comes with a generous 32GB of DDR5 6400 RAM, a $500 item in the current PC building economy. You also get a 2TB SSD gen 4 SSD, Wi-Fi 6, and Bluetooth 5.3.

If you'd prefer to go for a gaming-focused CPU, this 9800X3D PC comes with an RTX 5070 12GB, 32GB of DDR5, and a 1TB SSD instead.

#10541. Hunter is killing Google Home, Alexa controls for its smart ceiling fans

Hunter, a major brand for ceiling fans in the US, is pulling the plug on its Google Home and Amazon Alexa bridge for controlling “HunterSMART” models in just a few weeks.

Effective January 1, 2027, Hunter will pull support for the cloud-reliant “HunterSMART” connection used for Google Home and Amazon Alexa controls. Hunter’s app and the included remote will continue to work, but connections to smart home ecosystems will not.

Beginning January 1, 2027, the legacy cloud-to-cloud connections used for app linking between the HunterSMART™ mobile apps and Google Home and/or Amazon Alexa will no longer be supported.

After this date, Google home and Alexa functionality that relies on this legacy cloud connection will no longer work for HunterSMART fans, including voice commands, scenes, and automations.

This bridge is going away in favor of a Matter connection, with Hunter having been upgrading select models to be Matter-compatible over the past year. But this is only available to “eligible” models, not everyone who was already using Hunter’s existing bridge. A list of compatible models is available on Hunter’s website, with firmware updates rolling out via the HunterSMART app.

Some models, such as the one I reviewed a few years ago, require updated hardware. Besides that, the one I have can’t even be re-connected to Wi-Fi to check for an update as Hunter’s Android app consistently crashes during the setup process.

Once the fan has been updated, it won’t be available via the existing link and will instead require re-pairing via your smart home app of choice, whether that’s Google Home or another.

…Hunter is transitioning eligible HunterSMART™ products to Matter, an industry standard designed to support broader smart-home compatibility.

If your fan is eligible for Matter-compatibility, you will be able to continue using all smart functionality within the Google Home and/or Alexa ecosystems after completing the applicable Matter migration & setup.

Once your HunterSMART fan is Matter-enabled, connecting your fan to Google and Alexa will work differently than it does today. Instead of linking your HunterSMART account to Google Home or Alexa, you will need to setup your Matter-enabled fan directly into these compatible ecosystems using the Matter setup available via the Google Home and/or Alexa apps. Matter-enabled devices do not use the legacy cloud linking service for smart controls that is being discontinued.

How can I migrate my HunterSMART fan to Matter?

Depending on your fan model and hardware configuration, a firmware update or updated hardware components may be required for Matter compatibility. Available Matter upgrade options and instructions will vary by product. You can visit our HunterSMART™ Matter resource page for the latest information about Matter compatibility, available upgrade options, and next steps.

#10539. How Text-to-Image AI Generators Work, and 5 Tools That Deliver Good Results

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Type “a lighthouse on a cliff at dusk, oil painting, warm light” into a text box, wait ten seconds, and a picture that never existed appears. It feels like magic, but it is a well-understood pipeline of language models, neural networks and a great deal of math. Knowing how that pipeline works makes you a better prompter, helps you diagnose bad results, and makes it easier to choose the right tool for a job.

This guide explains how text-to-image generators turn words into pixels, why they fail in predictable ways, and how to write prompts that work with the model instead of against it. It then compares five tools that consistently produce good results, starting with Pollo AI, and finishes with a practical workflow, a troubleshooting table and an FAQ. Plans, credits and model versions change often, so confirm current details on each vendor’s site before you commit to a subscription.

Early image generators were mostly GANs, or generative adversarial networks. A GAN pits two networks against each other: a generator that produces images and a discriminator that tries to spot fakes. GANs could produce sharp faces and textures, but they were hard to train, often collapsed into repeating the same few outputs, and were poor at following free-form text.

A second line of research used autoregressive models, which generate an image piece by piece the way a language model generates words. These handled text prompts better but were slow at high resolution.

The shift came with diffusion models. Researchers showed that a network trained to reverse a gradual noising process could generate images with excellent quality and diversity, and that it was far more stable to train than a GAN. Combine that with text conditioning and a compressed image representation, and you get the systems behind most of today’s tools. Newer models increasingly swap the traditional convolutional network for transformers, but the core idea of iterative denoising is still the foundation.

Under the hood, mainstream generators combine three ideas: a way to understand text, a way to represent images compactly, and a way to generate them gradually. The sections below follow a prompt from keystroke to finished picture.

Neural networks cannot read words, so your prompt is first split into tokens, small chunks of text such as whole words or word fragments. A text encoder then converts those tokens into an embedding, a grid of numbers that captures meaning and context. Many systems use encoders trained jointly on images and their captions, so that the text “a red bicycle” and pictures of red bicycles end up close together in the same mathematical space. Some newer systems add a large language model to understand longer, more detailed instructions.

This is why wording matters. The encoder only sees what you wrote, and it has a limited capacity. A short, specific prompt produces a clear signal. A long, rambling one dilutes each instruction.

Instead of drawing on a blank canvas, the model begins with random static. The pattern of that static is controlled by a seed number. The same prompt with a different seed gives a different image, because it starts from different noise. If a tool lets you fix the seed, you can make results repeatable and change just one variable at a time.

During training, engineers take millions of real images and add noise to them in small steps until only static remains. The network is then trained to predict the noise that was added at each step, which teaches it to reverse the process. At generation time, the model applies that skill to pure noise, removing a little at each step. Because it has learned what real images look like, the “noise” it removes gradually reveals something that looks plausible.

Your text embedding is fed into the network through a mechanism called cross-attention. At each step, parts of the evolving image can “look at” relevant parts of the prompt and adjust accordingly. A region that will become a lighthouse attends to the word “lighthouse”. A region that will become the sky attends to “dusk”.

A setting called classifier-free guidance, often shown as guidance scale or prompt strength, controls how strongly each step is pulled toward the prompt. The model computes one prediction with the prompt and one without it, then exaggerates the difference. A low value gives the model freedom and often more natural, varied results. A high value follows the words more literally but can produce oversaturated, over-sharpened or distorted images.

Running diffusion directly on full-resolution pixels would be very expensive. The idea behind Stable Diffusion, known as latent diffusion, is to compress images into a much smaller latent representation using an autoencoder. Denoising happens in that compact space, and a decoder turns the final latent back into a full image. This is a major reason high-quality generation now runs in seconds on a cloud GPU instead of minutes.

The number of denoising steps and the scheduler, which decides how much noise to remove at each step, shape the speed and look of the result. More steps usually add detail up to a point, then give diminishing returns. Many tools hide these settings, but the trade-off is always the same: fewer steps are faster and cheaper, more steps can look more refined.

Most pipelines finish with an upscaler or refinement pass that sharpens detail and fixes small artifacts. Many tools also offer inpainting, which regenerates only a selected region, outpainting, which extends the canvas, and variations, which restart from a related image.

A generator can only produce what it has learned to represent. Models are trained on very large collections of images paired with captions, and the quality, diversity and licensing of that data affect both the output and the legal picture. Models trained on more photographs tend to produce convincing photorealism. Models tuned on illustration and concept art lean toward painterly looks.

This also explains a few behaviors. Common subjects look great because the model has seen thousands of examples. Rare or very specific subjects, such as an unusual machine part or a niche cultural item, often look generic or wrong. Biases in the data can show up as stereotypes, for example in how “a doctor” or “a CEO” is depicted, so it helps to describe the people and setting you actually want.

Understanding the process explains the familiar failures:

Pollo AI’s text to image AI is a browser-based generator built around a simple loop: write a prompt, pick a style, generate, and download. Its page highlights HD output and a wide range of looks, from photorealistic scenes to cute cartoons, anime, pencil sketches, isometric art, abstract compositions and surreal visuals. Files can be downloaded as WebP, PNG or JPEG, a practical spread for web work: WebP keeps pages light, PNG preserves clean edges and transparency-friendly graphics, and JPEG works almost everywhere.

Pollo is also part of a wider platform that covers AI video, so a still image you like can become the starting point for motion without switching ecosystems. That matters for teams producing social content, because one workflow can cover both the image and the short clip built from it.

Things to check: the page does not list specific model names or credit amounts, so review the current plan details before relying on it for high-volume work. Commercial use requires a paid plan.

Best for: quick, versatile image generation across many styles, especially if you also plan to make video.

Midjourney has built its reputation on strong aesthetics. Images tend to arrive with polished composition, dramatic lighting and a distinctive painterly feel, which is why it is popular for concept art, mood boards, album-style visuals and editorial illustration. It offers style references and personalization, so you can steer results toward a consistent look across a project. It also supports editing and variation tools for refining a chosen image.

The trade-offs are practical. It is a paid service, and its prompts reward experimentation over literal instruction following, so you sometimes get something beautiful that is not exactly what you described.

Best for: artistic, high-impact visuals where mood matters more than exact specification.

Adobe Firefly focuses on professional workflows and commercial confidence. Adobe describes Firefly’s own models as trained on licensed content such as Adobe Stock and public-domain material, and it attaches Content Credentials metadata that records AI involvement. It connects with tools many teams already use, including Photoshop features like generative fill and generative expand, which suits designers who edit as much as they generate.

Firefly also provides controls such as style and composition references. Its results can feel cleaner and more commercial than Midjourney’s, which is an advantage for marketing assets and a limitation if you want a wild artistic look.

Best for: marketing and design teams that need editing tools and clearer licensing.

Ideogram is known for rendering legible text inside images, a task where many generators struggle. That makes it a strong choice for posters, social graphics, logo concepts, packaging mockups and product visuals that need a headline or short phrase to read correctly. It also offers style controls and a community gallery that doubles as a prompt library.

Even so, long paragraphs of on-image text can still go wrong. Keep the copy to a few words, put it in quotation marks in the prompt, and describe the lettering style.

Best for: graphics that combine imagery with short, readable text.

Leonardo AI is built for creators who want more control. It offers multiple models, fine-tuning options, image-to-image guidance and a canvas for editing, with a particular following among game artists and designers who need consistent characters and assets. Features such as reference-based generation help keep a character recognizable across many scenes.

The wide set of options is powerful but means a steeper learning curve than a one-box generator, and credit systems can take time to understand.

Best for: consistent characters, game and concept assets, and users who like tuning settings.

A reliable prompt has five parts. Put the most important part first, because it often carries the most weight.

How do text-to-image generators work? They convert your prompt into a numerical representation, then use a diffusion model to remove noise from a random starting image step by step, guided by that representation, until a matching picture appears.

Which text-to-image tool is best? It depends on the job. Pollo AI suits fast, varied generation, Midjourney suits artistic work, Adobe Firefly suits commercial design, Ideogram suits images with text and Leonardo AI suits tuned, consistent assets.

Why does the same prompt give different results? Each run starts from a different random seed. Fixing the seed, where a tool allows it, makes results more repeatable.

Can I use AI-generated images commercially? Often yes, but the rules differ by tool and plan. Pollo, for example, notes that commercial use requires a paid plan. Always check the current terms.

How do I get readable text in an AI image? Use a tool strong at text, keep the wording to a few words, and add the text in a design editor if accuracy is critical.

What file format should I download? WebP for lightweight web pages, PNG for graphics and sharp edges, JPEG for broad compatibility.

Can I turn a generated image into a video? Many platforms support it. Pollo AI, for example, also covers AI video, so you can move from a still to motion in one place.

Text-to-image generators work by turning language into numbers and using those numbers to guide a model that denoises random static into a picture. Once you understand that, prompts stop being guesswork: you give the model a clear subject, style and lighting, you iterate one change at a time, and you fix small flaws locally. Start with a versatile option such as Pollo AI, then add Midjourney, Firefly, Ideogram or Leonardo when a project needs their particular strengths.

#10538. Apple should have made the MacBook logo RGB

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

The PROLED MacBook Pro’s logo is lit with boring white LEDs. Missed opportunity.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

The PROLED MacBook Pro’s logo is lit with boring white LEDs. Missed opportunity.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

When Apple CEO John Ternus announced the first touchscreen Mac last month, the biggest reaction in the room wasn’t for the PROLED MacBook Pro’s OLED touchscreen or the thinner and lighter chassis; it was for the illuminated lid logo. It’s a throwback to the light-up logos on the old MacBooks, which Apple started phasing out in 2015, and which were completely gone by the 2018 MacBook Air redesign. It’s a nostalgia play, but boy did it work. It’s fun!

Just like in the old days, the new logo’s brightness is linked to the screen’s: The brighter or dimmer the screen, the brighter or dimmer the logo. But the light-up logo on the old MacBooks was illuminated by the screen’s backlight, and an OLED display doesn’t have one. Thanks to iFixit, we now know it’s lit by four white LEDs and synced with the screen’s brightness by software. But that means Apple could have used RGB LEDs.

Red, orange, yellow, green, blue, indigo, violet, or anything in between? On the lid of your new MacBook Pro? Why not? After all, Apple insists it’s still serious about gaming, and nothing says “serious about gaming” like RGB lighting.

I was secretly hoping users would be able to customize the illuminated lid cutout much like the Xbox logo on Microsoft’s Elite Series 2 controllers. But alas, no luck. You can turn off the illumination with some Terminal commands or take control of it with a third-party app. But when it’s lit, it’s only lit with white light. And iFixit’s teardown also showed the difficulty of accessing those LEDs (the screen delaminated and broke in the process). So we’re unlikely to see LED RGB mods for it unless someone’s brave enough to void their warranty and risk a pricey screen repair on their $3,000 laptop.

The most fun you can have with the Apple lid logo is to install a free third-party app like LogoLight and have it pulse, breathe, match the beat of any music you’re playing, or flash some Morse code. There’s some fun there, but if you combined these features with a splash of color we’d really be cooking.

MacBooks aren’t gaming laptops, so RGB lighting may seem like a mismatch, but multicolor LEDs could have been a cool Easter egg. Nobody thought Apple would put a touchscreen on a MacBook either, and the iPhone Duo and HomeView also show Apple is in a new age of experimentation during the early Ternus era. Maybe if the light-up lid logo ever comes to future MacBook Airs or a MacBook Neo, Apple could cut loose a little.

#10537. Googlebook apps built by Googlers add macOS-like status bar shortcuts, keyboard commands

Googlebooks are a bit starved of desktop-class features at launch, but one of Google’s pitches around this platform is how apps can be built for it, and the company’s own employees are showing off what that looks like with some clever apps that just so happen to add features that feel a lot like they’re replicating macOS staples.

In a Reddit thread, three Googlebook apps built by Googlers through Antigravity are shown off, and they’re pretty cool, regardless of how you might feel about the platform itself.

“BentoBox” is a utility that gives you control over the status bar on Googlebook, enabling you to fill the status bar with shortcuts, mini-apps, and more. It’s full of glancable information such as weather conditions, battery charging speeds, and more, but also includes useful tools such as being able to access a clipboard history, set a timer, and more. The app requires permissions including accessibility overlays, but it works like a charm.

I had no idea the status bar on Googlebook could even be used like this, and it really makes that space a lot more useful. Of course, you have to draw a comparison here to macOS, which also uses this space for app shortcuts and tools, though BentoBox offers a different type of customization.

BentoBox is available for download via Google Play for free.

The other Mac-like app here is “Booklight,” a launcher that’s basically Apple’s Spotlight, just for Googlebook. It has a transparent design and lets you search “apps, settings, and the web.”

Technically, Googlebook already can do this in its built-in search bar (in the app drawer), but Booklight has more customization and functionality, including being able to write out shorthand commands all in one sentence with the app then completing the command in the appropriate apps, as well as having a clearer UI for experiences such as typing in quick math equations. It’s also good at finding symbols (so you don’t have to remember the U+ codes that carried over from ChromeOS). Not one I found myself wanting to use, but the potential is there.

Both BentoBox and Booklight were developed by Alexander Kuscher, VP over Laptops & Tablets at Google.

The other app highlighted in Google’s thread was Wayfinder, a file manager app described as a “desktop-grade file manager.” The big bonus here is network file access for NAS boxes and other uses, but the app also just has a more advanced UX compared to Googlebook’s built-in Files app.

Googlebook’s app gap is a real problem at launch, but these apps do show there’s a lot of potential in the platform, it’s just a matter of getting developers on board – which itself will require Google and its partners to actually sell some of these laptops… classic chicken-and-egg situation.

Japanese workers dealing with the impacts of Microsoft's ongoing 365 outage have reportedly taken to analog measures, including using fax machines to deal with the ongoing blackout, the Japan Times reports. An outage that has now lasted nearly 24 hours was still going on Friday when employees turned up to work.

The downtime has meant businesses reliant on Microsoft 365 are unable to use Outlook, Teams, and Copilot, with workplaces hit with error messages and blank screens, forced to reschedule meetings or delay work. For some, the outage has proven a respite from Japan's notoriously workaholic culture. "I couldn’t open Outlook and panicked … I got in touch with my boss and was told to stay home," one person reported on X, while another remarked that the only task they could carry out at work was to clean the coffee machine in the production room.

But while some employees and companies are throwing in the towel and calling it an early weekend, others are taking a novel, more analog approach. "Microsoft’s server glitch has made it impossible to get any work done since this morning. I guess phones and fax machines really are unbeatable. … We’re going full analog!" one user claimed. The report cites another employee who confirmed that their company was switching to fax machines to send the regular updates the company normally sends out as PDFs. "Analog is strong," they rejoiced.

Some 78% of Japanese companies rely on Microsoft for their digital infrastructure. The outage began on Thursday, with mass disruption to Outlook, Teams, Copilot, OneDrive, and Microsoft 365 as a whole. It's a saga impacting entire sectors, including UK health care, businesses, travel, and transport.

Microsoft's latest update said it was seeing "encouraging recovery progress" in Europe, while the situation in America was improving, but warned some users may still experience intermittent issues. The company has also denied rumblings that the outage was caused by a cybersecurity breach. The outage has even caused some companies, such as Switzerland's luxury Richemont (Cartier) outfit, to delay giving their latest earnings report.

#10532. Fitbit Sense & Versa losing 3rd-party watch faces this month, existing downloads remain

Google announced today that Fitbit Sense and Versa smartwatches will soon lose the ability to “install or update third-party apps and clocks.”

This change to the Fitbit App Gallery applies to the Versa, Versa 2, Versa 3, Versa 4, Sense, and Sense 2.

Existing “third-party apps or clock faces installed on your Fitbit device” will remain available, but you can no longer download new ones (or update them) starting November 28. As such, “if you choose to delete an app or clock face, or factory reset your device, you will no longer be able to re-install it.”

Instead, the Fitbit App Gallery will “exclusively feature apps and clock faces created by Fitbit and Google.”

The November 28 deadline also applies to third-party developers. Google will “no longer support uploading new apps and clock faces or updating existing ones.”

Google winding down Fitbit OS for smartwatches is not particularly surprising as the company has been all-in on Wear OS and the Pixel Watch. That said, the Sense 2 and Versa 4 should probably no longer be on sale.

Former Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang has reportedly called himself “a piece of sh**" as he left the company he built over 33 years. But despite his resignation and the controversies surrounding him, it seems that Nvidia employees are still rallying behind him, with thousands of comments on the company’s internal “suggestion box” showing support for the disgraced leader, according to Wired.

“If you believe in the mission, bring back THE boss,” one commenter said, while another one wrote, “We are all humans and can make mistakes. Jensen did so many good things and he needs a second chance.” An Nvidia staffer told Wired privately, “Nvidia is Nvidia because of Jensen,” even as another employee sent a company-wide email demanding transparency into his resignation. “I stand by Jensen. Not because I believe any person is infallible, but because I refuse to believe that standing for fairness must mean standing at a distance from someone whose leadership has meant so much to us.” They also added, “If America fails the man who helped build its future, then this world does not deserve Jensen Huang.”

Jensen’s interactions with Ms. Huang are not unusual or unexpected. Meetings with customers are part of a CEO's job; Jensen talks with thousands of customer executives every year,” an Nvidia spokesperson told Tom's Hardware. However, things took a turn for the worse for the Nvidia CEO when reports came out that he actually had an affair with Ms. Huang.

Nvidia General Counsel and Secretary of the Board Tim Teter confronted Jensen at his house when this came out, after which he confessed. The founder soon released a statement admitting to and apologizing for his actions, while the company’s board formed an independent committee to investigate. However, another scandal broke out revealing that the former Nvidia chief had another affair — this time with a subordinate. This was apparently the last straw for the board, and the Nvidia co-founder was forced to resign soon after.

Reports say that Jensen Huang was a hands-on leader, with over 60 executives at the company reporting directly to him, and that he was known to respond to emails quickly, whether it was day or night. But, more importantly, he was the face of the company who was particularly loved by many people, giving rise to the term “Jensanity” in his home country of Taiwan. Regardless of his status as a billionaire, people still find him trying out street food, eating at fast food chains, and even honoring his humble beginnings as a busboy at Denny’s.

Aside from these latest scandals, many gamers castigate Huang for abandoning the core market that supported Nvidia before it became an AI giant, with consumer graphics cards now drastically more expensive than ever before. He was also often criticized for his all-out push on artificial intelligence and stance on selling chips to China. Still, those issues did not detract from his aura, with his trademark leather jacket raising nearly $1 million at a charity auction. It remains to be seen what will happen to Jensen after his monumental fall from grace, with SpaceX and Tesla mogul Elon Musk noting it would be an honor to hire Huang.

#10528. Building Reliable Product Emails Into Your Application

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

A password reset email is part of your login flow. An invoice notification is part of your billing experience. Yet development teams often treat these messages as background tasks: render a template, call a sending API, and move on. That approach leaves an important part of the product experience untested until a user cannot complete the action they started.

Testing the application interface alone leaves a gap. A successful request to an email provider does not establish that the recipient can find the message, understand it, or use its links. Product emails need explicit success criteria, useful diagnostic records, and recovery options when something fails.

A reliable workflow combines application tests, controlled mailbox checks, and pre-send diagnostics for authentication and inbox placement. A comparison of available tools can help you identify which checks your existing setup covers and where additional testing would be useful. The goal is to investigate specific gaps rather than add another service without a clear purpose.

Start with the action the recipient needs to complete. A weekly activity summary can tolerate a delay that would make a sign-in code useless. An invoice needs accurate amounts and a clear payment destination. A security alert needs enough context for someone to recognize the event without exposing sensitive information.

For each message, document its trigger, expected timing, required data, and expiration behavior. Decide what the application should display if submission fails or the user requests another copy. These decisions give developers and testers concrete acceptance criteria.

For a password reset, test the complete journey: requesting the message, receiving it in a controlled mailbox, following the link, and setting a new password. Also check what happens when the link expires or has already been used.

Template tests should cover the data your application actually encounters. Try long names, missing optional fields, non-Latin characters, and large order totals. Escape user-provided values appropriately, and make sure conditional sections disappear cleanly when their data is absent.

Inspect both HTML and plain-text versions. Instructions should remain understandable when images are blocked, while descriptive links and readable text should make the next action clear. Check that every URL points to the correct environment, especially when staging and production share template code.

A development sandbox lets you inspect generated messages without contacting customers. Use it to check headers, attachments, and content. Keep a separate rendering check for the email clients your audience uses, because a sandbox preview cannot reproduce every client’s behavior.

A background queue can prevent a slow sending API from delaying a web request. It also creates another failure boundary: a worker may time out after the provider has accepted the email but before your application records the result.

If the job retries blindly, the recipient may receive two messages. Assign a stable identifier to each logical notification and record the provider’s message identifier when available. Where supported, reuse an idempotency key for retries of the same send operation, following the provider’s documented retention window.

Distinguish retries from intentional resends. A user asking for a new reset link is a different event from a worker repeating an uncertain request. Define which link remains valid when another is issued, and test that behavior explicitly.

Retries also need limits. Temporary service failures may justify another attempt with backoff. A permanently invalid address needs a correction or suppression workflow, rather than repeated sending.

Provider events describe different stages of the sending process. An acceptance response means the provider accepted the request. A delivery event generally means the receiving mail server accepted the message; it does not prove inbox placement or that a person read it.

Capture the events your provider exposes, including bounces, delays, and complaints. Associate them with the original notification so support staff can investigate a particular report. Confirm each event’s documented meaning instead of treating similar labels across providers as interchangeable.

Webhook handlers should verify incoming requests using the provider’s documented mechanism and tolerate duplicate notifications. Avoid assuming events always arrive in chronological order. Record enough history to explain what happened without allowing a late event to overwrite a more informative state.

Before releasing a significant template or infrastructure change, send controlled messages through the production sending configuration to test accounts at commonly used mailbox providers. Use synthetic data, and check placement, rendering, link behavior, and how long the message takes to arrive.

Inspect authentication results in the received headers. Published DNS records are only part of the configuration; the actual message must use the expected signing domain and sending infrastructure. If you change providers or domains, repeat these checks rather than assuming the previous results still apply.

Treat test mailbox results as samples. They can reveal problems, but they cannot guarantee identical placement for every recipient. Mailbox settings, filtering decisions, and recipient history can produce different outcomes.

Provide a resend option with sensible rate limits and explain when users should expect the message. Where appropriate, let signed-in users verify or correct their address. For public account-recovery forms, avoid responses that reveal whether a particular address has an account.

Give support staff a way to distinguish a notification that was never generated from a failed submission or a message accepted by the receiving server. Protect that diagnostic access, limit retention, and avoid logging reset tokens, private message bodies, or unnecessary personal information.

Reliable product email depends on the whole journey, from the triggering event to the recipient’s next action. Testing each stage and making failures observable helps teams resolve problems before users repeatedly click resend or abandon the task.

#10527. This tiny gamepad with dual analog thumbsticks pushes the limits of portable controllers

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Larger hands may struggle with the Atom Plus, but it squeezes a lot of functionality into an extremely tiny controller.

Larger hands may struggle with the Atom Plus, but it squeezes a lot of functionality into an extremely tiny controller.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

If you buy something from a link, The Verge may earn a commission. See our ethics statement.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

As much as I appreciate the ingenious design of the Nintendo Switch’s Joy-Con, my family and I can’t stand using them as standalone gamepads. So when we travel with the Switch 2, we always bring at least one full-size gamepad, plus a couple of 8BitDo’s Zero 2 controllers for games that don’t use analog thumbsticks. I’ve spent years hunting for an alternative that pairs the size of the Zero 2 with as much of the functionality of a larger controller as I can get, and CRKD’s new Atom Plus is nearly exactly that. But if you have larger hands like I do, you may find the gamepad’s focus on portability a struggle.

Measuring around 3.5 inches wide, the $29.99 Atom Plus is about a half-inch smaller than the 8BitDo Zero 2 but squeezes in considerably more features and functionality. The most obvious is a pair of symmetrical analog thumbsticks with anti-drift tunneling magnetoresistance technology. You also get shoulder buttons and triggers, rumble feedback, a dedicated home button, motion controls, and customization through a mobile app.

Like many third-party controllers, the Atom Plus works with multiple platforms, including the Switch and Switch 2, PCs, Android, iOS, and various smart TVs. And just like other gamepads, pairing to different devices requires putting the Atom Plus into one of several Bluetooth modes by holding down a combination of buttons at power-up, which in this case is the home button plus a specific direction on the D-pad. It’s easy to do, but never easy to remember: I frequently needed to grab the manual or quickly Google which direction I need to press when switching devices. The Atom Plus already features a bunch of technical info etched into the back of the controller, so I’d love to see these important pairing instructions included there, too. (And that goes for all multiplatform gamepads.)

I primarily tested the Atom Plus with the Nintendo Switch 2 since I mostly plan to use the controller as a travel accessory. The thumbsticks are about the same size as those on the original Switch Joy-Con, which means they’re smaller than the Switch 2’s. And while I prefer an asymmetrical layout, the Atom Plus’ sticks are very good, with a little more tension than Nintendo’s that I find actually helps with precision movements given their limited travel.

While the overall build quality feels excellent, the Atom Plus’ tiny footprint creates some compromises and challenges. The front action buttons and D-pad feel nice and squishy, but the gamepad’s controls are a little crowded. When pressing down or right on the D-pad, my thumb regularly bumps the left thumbstick. It doesn’t hinder gameplay, but I do find it an annoying distraction.

The shoulder buttons use digital microswitches, as do the triggers, which means the latter can’t be used for racing games that support analog acceleration controls. I like that the two sets of buttons on the top edge sit at slightly different heights, but I would have preferred a little more differentiation since I’ve occasionally accidentally pressed the triggers when I meant to hit the shoulder buttons.

I was impressed with the Atom Plus’ customizability, however. When paired to an accompanying mobile app, you can check the controller’s remaining charge, turn off rumble feedback, see its unique product number (by tapping your phone to an NFC chip inside the controller), and remap any of its controls. That includes the pair of dual-function shortcut levers that replicate the Switch’s plus and minus buttons and screenshot shortcut.

Those levers move up and down and cleverly reduce the number of buttons crowding the front of the Atom Plus, but they feel a little too small, which is more or less the general theme. It’s a tiny gamepad that’s great for travel or stashing in your everyday carry. But, unsurprisingly, it probably won’t find its way into your regular gaming rotation. It’s assumed that players will be willing to settle for the compromise of occasionally contorting their fingers in weird, new ways for one of the smallest fully featured controllers on the market.

I don’t think a dual-stick controller could get any smaller than the Atom Plus without making too many ergonomic compromises. So while I applaud CRKD for the careful balancing act behind the Atom Plus’ design, and would certainly recommend it for those wanting a very capable controller that’s also cheap and portable, I’m now curious how it compares to Microsoft’s recently announced Xbox Nano controller that’s “surprisingly comfortable,” according to The Verge’s Tom Warren. It’s $20 more expensive than CRKD’s and noticeably larger, but it adds analog triggers, a more spacious design, and stubby grips making it easier to hold for those of us with beefy mitts.

#10521. Samsung Galaxy S27 series camera specs leak, and they’re disappointing

New leaks reveal the camera specs for Samsung’s upcoming Galaxy S27 series which includes three lenses across the lineup, but not the upgrades you want, nor what Ultra owners might expect.

SammyGuru reports that Samsung is “evaluating” camera setups for the Galaxy S27, S27+, S27 Pro, and S27 Ultra, and it looks like a mixed bag across the board.

The base Galaxy S27 and S27+ are looking very familiar, with identical main camera sensors to last year, and the same ultrawide sensor that’s been in use since the Galaxy S23 series. The telephoto and selfie sensors are looking at new hardware, but without any major spec upgrades.

Galaxy S27 Pro and Ultra, meanwhile, have a new 200MP main camera on deck, a 50MP ultrawide shooter, and a mere 12MP telephoto. The Ultra might get 5x compared to the Pro’s 3x optical zoom, but with a secondary telephoto lens going missing, existing users of the lineup are sure to be disappointed.

Notably, SammyGuru’s article mentions the Pro as using “the Galaxy S26 Ultra’s 3x sensor,” but shows the 12MP Samsung S5K3LD in that place instead. We’re assuming that’s a typo seeing as the post mentions the Pro as using the Samsung S5KJN3 or the Sony IMX855 earlier on, but regardless, the Pro only gets 3x optical zoom to the Ultra’s 5x.

Some of these specs have been mentioned by other sources such as GalaxyClub and leaker Ice Universe, but this is the most complete picture of the S27’s camera plans we’ve seen to date.

Personally, I’m very “whelmed” on this one. As RAMageddon rages on and Samsung hunts for new ways to balance specs vs price, cameras are a place that’s obviously going to be cut, but competitors are already running laps around Samsung on the hardware front. Cutting lenses and skimping out on upgrades (again) doesn’t help matters.

#10520. Malaysia and Singapore step up Nvidia AI chip probes amidst Jensen Huang controversy

Does it mean, illegal activities won't happen again?

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Malaysia and Singapore have intensified scrutiny of AI hardware shipments amid concerns about the potential circumvention of U.S. export restrictions on China. Malaysia has launched an investigation into whether AI hardware imported by a subsidiary of the ill-fated Megaspeed was subsequently exported to other countries, reports Reuters. Meanwhile, Singapore has charged another executive over questionable purchases of 917 servers that may contain Nvidia accelerators, according to The Straits Times. The news follows the resignation of Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang over claims he had multiple extra-marital affairs, including with a possible Chinese intelligence asset who facilitated GPU smuggling into China. Analysts speculate that systematic chip smuggling may be the reason behind his departure.

Malaysia's Ministry of Investment, Trade and Industry is examining whether Speedmatrix, the Malaysian subsidiary of Singapore-based Megaspeed, re-exported any of the AI processors and servers it had imported for use in local data centers. Apparently, after months of investigating, authorities have yet to find out whether any such shipments occurred and, if they did, whether they complied with Malaysian laws. Malaysia introduced restrictions on the export, transit, and trans-shipment of advanced U.S.-developed AI accelerators in July 2025, so it now requires companies to obtain appropriate permits.

The Malaysian ministry said it was cooperating with customs authorities as well as officials in Singapore and the U.S., and could introduce additional restrictions. Prime Minister Anwar Ibrahim said there was no evidence that Megaspeed-related AI hardware had left Malaysia, but he promised to cooperate with international investigations.

Speaking of Megaspeed, the company said on November 12 that it had hired a U.S. law firm to conduct a comprehensive inventory of its AI GPUs and independently assess its compliance with U.S. export-control regulations, reports DigiTimes. The company also confirmed that it had cooperated with inspections by the U.S. Department of Commerce's Bureau of Industry and Security (BIS) and was prepared to cooperate with other U.S. government agencies if necessary.

James Tan Yong Pong, chief executive of Megaspeed, stressed that compliance with U.S. export rules as well as transparent operations were always a priority for the company. However, Megaspeed did not disclose how many GPUs it owns and which GPU models it has, what BIS examined during its inspections, or whether the authorities had identified any violations.

Meanwhile, Singaporean authorities have brought 41 criminal charges against Shi Bingzhe, a 34-year-old Chinese citizen who served as director of Superphi and chief operating officer of NJ Global Financial. The two companies purchased 917 servers containing restricted Nvidia hardware from Aperia International in 41 transactions between May 2024 and February 2025, according to prosecutors. Authorities allege that the companies had reason to believe the servers were linked to criminal activity by Aperia International. Superphi now faces six charges, while NJ Global Financial faces 35.

The latest charges expand a case that now involves five individuals and six companies. Four firms — Aperia International, A-Speed Infotech, Aperia Cloud Services (II), and Luxuriate Your Life — were charged in July over allegedly making false claims when purchasing servers. Meanwhile, the exact nature of those claims was not disclosed in the report. Authorities have frozen the disposal of a property worth approximately S$55 million and confiscated around S$1 million from bank accounts. Shi faces up to 10 years in prison and a fine of S$500,000 per charge if convicted. His next court appearance is scheduled for January 4, 2027.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

The two cases largely highlight the significance of Malaysia and Singapore in the supply chain of restricted AI hardware to places where it should not be, such as China. Singapore's investigation focuses on alleged fraud and transactions involving proceeds of criminal activity, whereas Malaysia seeks to find out whether imported AI equipment was subsequently shipped abroad in violation of local regulations.

Neither investigation has found that the hardware ultimately reached China, but both illustrate difficulties that authorities face as they attempt to enforce restrictions on advanced AI hardware across international supply chains.
