A traditional bed slinger made mostly of LEGO; the printbed, leadscrews, and even the gantry are LEGO.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Adam Turaj, a Mechatronics Engineering student at the University of Waterloo, built an open-source LEGO 3D printer named “Studprint” with only 13 pieces of the printer not made out of LEGO. The bill of materials only totals $173.35, with $30 of the budget going to LEGO pieces that comprise 92% of the whole build. Turaj’s project got him flown to the Rocky Mountain RepRap Festival 2026, where he got to meet Prusa CMO Zack Freedman and presented Studprint to over 4,000 people at the event.

Earlier this year, Hack Club posted a challenge called Infill, calling makers and hobbyists to design their own 3D printers from scratch and receive a $350 grant to build their printers. Whoever submits a benchy with their printer gets a chance to be flown to the Rocky Mountain RepRap Festival 2026, and Turaj was one of them.

Turaj joined this challenge, designing his LEGO 3D printer in SOLIDWORKS. He named the project Studprint, referring to the studs on a LEGO brick. What takes this project even farther is that Turaj decided he would only use “classical LEGO,” avoiding LEGO Technic pieces wherever possible. Looking at the repository’s images of the CAD designs, you would easily mistake it for a virtual LEGO builder.

Turaj shared an image of the bill of materials (BOM) on the GitHub repository for his build, showing a final build price of $173.35. There are three major electronics components in the printer: the first is the BTT SKR Pico V1.0, a control board commonly used in open-source 3D printers like the Voron V0; for wireless capability, a Raspberry Pi Zero 2 W is used; and the whole printer is powered by a Mean Well LRS-150-24 switching power supply — with all three components priced at around $25 each. This printer is built with the backbone of a true DIY 3D-printer build, unlike this cute LEGO 3D printer we covered last year that used a 3D printing pen.

There are five NEMA 17 stepper motors, also popular in 3D printers, whether open- or closed-source. Four of the motors control the axes, while the fifth is used to feed filament to the Trianglelab V6 Extruder. The rest of the 3D printer is made of $30 worth of LEGO pieces; even the build plate is a block of LEGO. Turaj’s only print shown on the GitHub repository is a Benchy, sliced in Klipper and printed in 1.25 hours; it’s surprisingly good considering the leadscrews are all LEGO pieces.

#10480. Pixel Tag tidbits: Surprise NFC, Find Hub setup, & Bellroy keychain cases

The Made by Google 2026 lineup is finally complete with tomorrow’s retail launch of the Pixel Tag. You can find our review here, while there are some additional details about the device since the August announcement.

The Pixel Tag is the first Find Hub tracker with NFC. Google did not mention this connectivity specification in August, with Near Field Communication letting you tap the tracker to the back of your phone to immediately open device settings. The NFC area on the Pixel Tag is below the circular battery door, which you hold down and twist counterclockwise to open.

On Android 17 QPR2, tapping immediately opens the details page. On QPR1, you first get a system notification that you need to click. NFC joins Bluetooth 6.0 with Channel Sounding and Ultra Wideband (UWB).

The setup process adds a new screen after the initial pairing and terms of use. It lets you configure: Notify when left behind, Ring this phone, Edit name, Edit category, and Adjust volume for this tag.

The Pixel Tag has a physical button on the rear side below the battery cover. Clicking twice plays a loud sound on your phone if both devices are nearby. Once found, you can tap the button once to silence, or turn it off from your phone. This feature can be disabled from the Pixel Tag’s device details page on a tracker-by-tracker basis.

Google does not have a first-party case for the Pixel Tag. Instead, it’s working with Made for Google partner Bellroy.

There are two models available from the Google Store, with the first made of silicone. The Bellroy Lite Case Duo costs $24.99 and comes in a pack of two: Mist and Shadow colorways. It wraps everything but the face of the Pixel Tag (and its speaker cutout), while giving you a hole in the corner and color-matching cord.

There’s also the Bellroy Leather Tag Case in Artichoke, Azalea, Black, Pewter, and Red Ochre for $29.99.

#10479. Save serious money with RedPocket’s Black Friday plans that start at $5 a month

You’re not going to believe this, but there’s one part of your life that’s not actually getting more expensive. If you switch to RedPocket Mobile during its Black Friday sale, you can ditch your costly carrier and get a plan that starts at $5 per month. It’s not too good to be true: RedPocket has been around for 20 years, and unlike most MVNOs, it’s independently owned and operated. That gives it the ability to offer you real network choice when you sign up for a new plan (we’ll explain more later), and keeps your costs low. Really low.

But you’re reading this on 9to5Google, so how does it impact you as an Android user? That’s a great question.

RedPocket is offering $150 rebates on Android devices like the Pixel 11, Galaxy S26, Galaxy Z Flip 7, Moto G Play, and more. (You can also get $100 rebates for iPhones if you’re into that sort of thing.)

These offers run from November 9 through the 30th at redpocket.com, so don’t wait too long to save big.

It’s OK if you haven’t heard of RedPocket Mobile, because it has been quietly working for 20 years to improve the way people use their phones. Founded in 2006, the company works with all three major US carriers — AT&T, T-Mobile, and Verizon — and because it doesn’t own any of its towers, it can pass along significant savings to its customers. Here’s what the company says on its website:

RedPocket was built for price-conscious consumers who want real value and freedom from rigid, high-cost contracts. As a no-contract carrier, we’ve stayed focused on making reliable, transparent, and flexible phone service for everyone. Our plans are designed to fit real lifestyles, whether you’re a heavy data user, a frequent traveler, or just looking to cut your monthly bill.

Most MVNOs are owned by the Big Three, which means you don’t really have a choice of network when you sign up. But RedPocket works with all three major carriers and lets you choose which one works best for your location and usage patterns.

RedPocket offers a tool called CoverageGenius that helps you decide which network is right for you. While you can’t switch between networks once your line is set up, it’s a great way to figure out where to start.

I teased that $5/month cost at the beginning of the article, but here’s the real breakdown: you can get a full year of service for $60 if you’re a light data user. RedPocket’s Annual Essentials and Annual Plus plans get you unlimited calling and texting in the US, along with calling and data roaming in dozens of countries around the world. The main difference is how much data you get: 3GB for Essentials and 20GB for Plus.

Annual Premium ups the ante to unlimited data, plus calling and data roaming in even more countries.

Across all three plans, you’re saving between 33% and 50% off the regular price when you sign up between November 9th and 30th.

One thing to note about all of these plans: the annual prices are paid upfront, not billed monthly. Promotional pricing applies to the first year, and after that, annual plans renew at $110 for Essentials, $220 for Plus, and $330 for Premium.

RedPocket understands that it may be hard to commit to a full year of service even if it’s super cheap. That’s why it offers monthly plans, too, that can serve as a trial for any of the networks. It costs just $10 for the first month to try any of the three plans, with Monthly Plus rising to $20/month after that and Premium $30/month thereafter (Essentials stays at $10/month).

If you need a new phone to go along with your new plan, RedPocket is a great place to get it.

Jensen Huang once worked at Denny's as a seasonal dishwasher, busboy, and waiter.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Update 11/15/2026 6:40am PT: Denny's has restored the plaque commemorating the booth where Nvidia was founded. Denny's issued the following statement:“We’re reinstalling the plaque recognizing the conversations at Denny’s that helped launch NVIDIA.

Jensen Huang started at Denny’s as a dishwasher. Years later, he and his co-founders gathered in one of our booths to discuss the idea that became NVIDIA. Jensen Huang was one of the American success stories in Silicon Valley, where he had his humble beginnings as a seasonal dishwasher, busboy, and waiter at his nearby Denny’s before he rose to stardom as the head of Nvidia. The diner honored this achievement with a plaque at the booth where Jensen Huang, Chris Malachowsky, and Curtis Priem met after their working hours and first planned the inception of Nvidia. However, NBC News said that the sign has since been removed following Huang’s spectacular fall from grace and resignation from the company.

Unfortunately, employees say that the commemorative plaque, which read, “The booth that launched a $1 trillion company,” has been removed. It remains unclear who ordered the removal, though, so we reached out to Denny’s for comment and will update the story if they answer.

Despite being a billionaire, the former Nvidia CEO is known for keeping in touch with his roots. He has been spotted several times eating street food and enjoying local fast food in the various cities that he visits. He even once dragged the 35 Taiwanese industry chiefs to a restaurant in Taipei, earning that event the moniker “trillion-dollar banquet.” This “man of the people” persona has made him quite a popular figure among some people, fueling the Jensanity hype, especially in his home country of Taiwan.

We also saw this side of Jensen during the unveiling of the plaque in 2023, after Nvidia hit a $1 trillion valuation, when he balanced 10 plates and served food during the event. The restaurant also put up a food truck and served “Nvidia Breakfast Bytes” at GTC 2025, which consisted of four sausage links served with four buttermilk silver dollar pancakes and some syrup, and was available for a limited time for under $5 in all Denny’s branches across the country.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

This move is just another sign of Jensen’s sudden downfall. He was still the leader of one of the most valuable companies in the world a couple of weeks ago, evangelizing artificial intelligence to anyone willing to listen. Today finds a totally different reality, with sources saying that he looked “broken and shattered,” and he even called himself “a piece of sh--” as he left the company he built for over 30 years after the board ousted him.

There is no telling yet what’s next for Jensen Huang. Elon Musk has reportedly offered him a position, saying that it “would be an honor” to hire the person who was arguably one of the biggest driving forces in today’s AI industry, but he’s also facing numerous investigations from both his company, Congress, and the FBI. More importantly, Jensen will have to deal with the fallout of the revelations on his public image and personal life.

#10476. Google Fi brings back network switching with ‘secondary’ partner, expands W+ beyond Pixel

Google Fi Wireless is rolling out a big update today with the return of network switching thanks to a “secondary” partner.

Google Fi is now bringing back a second partner as part of its new “super network.” Most users will be on the “Primary network,” but Google Fi will automatically switch users to the “Secondary network” if it meaningfully improves performance with so-called “smart thresholds” for doing so. At launch, there is no manual switching.

Google is not naming the second network today, but did share that Ultra Wideband is supported. Officially, you are switching between “two of America’s fastest 5G networks.”

Network switching will first be supported on Pixel 9 and newer devices that have a Google Fi eSIM (and no other SIM active). It makes use of Android’s Multiple Enabled Profiles (MEP) eSIM technology that allows for two carrier profiles on a single chip.

It might be important to note that T-Mobile actually bought out Sprint, and added their towers to their network so its safe to say that Google Fi still works with the same coverage as before the merger.

The Google Fi app gains a “Your super network” card in the Home tab that shows whether you’re connected to the Primary or Secondary network. “See activity” lets you view switching history and when it last occurred. Google will also note if you’re in range of W+ networks.

Google today is also expanding its Wi-Fi Auto Connect+ network beyond Pixel phones to select Samsung Galaxy (S23, Fold/Flip 5, and newer) and modern Motorola phones. These premium Wi-Fi networks are available in millions of airports, malls, stores, and restaurants. The connection happens automatically, with the Google Fi Virtual Carrier Network encrypting data.

The final update today sees the companion app gain “Ask Fi.” This AI-powered chat is meant to quickly provide answers and guided troubleshooting for common issues. You can request human support with seamless handoff that keeps the chat context.

#10473. How to Use Developer Time Tracking Data to Improve Sprint Planning and Estimates

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Sprint estimates usually fail because development teams heavily rely on memory and assumptions instead of evidence. They assume similar tasks will take the same amount of time. Based on these half-baked assumptions, most managers plan around ideal sprint working hours. The backlog then fills with commitments that look realistic on paper but often end up delayed.

Actual development rarely follows that plan. There are always unexpected bugs and task dependencies that consume more time than teams account for in their estimates. Even the buffer time set aside for unexpected work may not be enough. This causes tasks to take longer than expected and pushes sprint commitments behind schedule.

Developer time tracking helps teams make better estimates with historical data. It shows how long previous tasks actually took, which provides managers with an evidence-based reference when they are planning similar work.

Reviewing this data before committing to a sprint scope turns estimation from guesswork into an evidence-based process.

Historical time data offers deeper insights, depending on how you collect and analyze it. Most importantly, it explains why planned work took longer than expected.

Before sprint planning, the following records should be reviewed.

Completed task duration: Check the time spent on finished tasks. Managers should compare similar work to understand typical development effort.

Actual versus estimated hours: Management should observe and identify differences between planned and recorded hours. If there are repeated delays, to some extent, it indicates inaccurate estimates.

Carryover work: Review unfinished tasks from previous sprints. Determine whether poor estimates or external delays caused the spillover..

Interruptions: Measure time spent on urgent requests and production issues. These activities reduce the development capacity that you had planned earlier.

Meetings and ceremonies: Review the time being spent on planning sessions and sprint reviews.

Bug-fix time: Examine hours spent resolving defects. You can also compare it to the time for developing new features.

Blocked tasks: Managers should account for the tasks delayed by missing approvals or technical dependencies.

Capacity differences: Compare team availability across sprints. Include leave and staffing changes in that time as well.

These records provide the most value when reviewed together. For example, a task may finish three days late even if the development work stayed within its estimated hours. The actual issue could be an external dependency.

Teams should understand these differences before using historical data to estimate future work.

Historical records provide a useful starting point for estimating new tasks. However, teams must interpret the data rather than simply copy previous estimates.

The following approaches can improve estimation accuracy.

The first step is to consider completed tasks that resemble upcoming work. Managers should compare their requirements and technical complexity. Moreover, the development environment should be considered.

For example, suppose a team needs to estimate a new API integration. Three previous integrations required 12, 15, and 18 hours of active work.

The average is 15 hours. This gives developers a reference point for the new estimate.

However, the average doesn't tell the entire story. The new integration may involve unfamiliar authentication methods or more complicated testing.

Review the reasons behind differences in previous task durations. Check whether developers encountered unexpected bugs or unclear requirements.

As a project manager, I'd consider these details before estimating for the next project. In my experience, they provide more context than an average alone.

Inaccurate estimates can happen; it doesn’t mean that there is a definite planning problem. However, repeated overruns across similar tasks may reveal an underlying issue.

Research suggests the pattern is widespread. A review of software estimation surveys reports that 60–80% of software projects encounter effort overruns, with the average overrun falling between 30% and 40%.

Start by calculating the difference between estimated and actual effort. Here is the formula for it.

Estimation variance (%) = [(Actual hours − Estimated hours) ÷ Estimated hours] × 100

Suppose a task was estimated at 10 hours but needed 14 hours. The actual effort exceeded the estimate by 40%.

You can compare the results across similar tasks.

For example, database migrations may repeatedly require more time than expected. This means that the team might be overlooking data validation or additional testing. Once the cause is clear, adjust future estimates accordingly.

Avoid increasing every estimate by the same percentage. Different types of work involve different risks. Teams can also break large tasks into smaller parts. This makes hidden development activities easier to identify.

The aim is to correct recurring estimation errors without unnecessarily increasing the entire sprint backlog.

Task duration doesn't always reflect the hours spent developing a feature.

A study of developer workdays by Meyer and colleagues, which tracked 2,197 hours of activity across 20 developers, found that coding accounted for around 21% of the typical day. Collaborative work such as meetings and email took up roughly 24%.

Consider a developer who starts a feature on Monday and finishes it on Wednesday. The task took three days to complete. This is pretty straightforward, and the estimation could be based on it; however, the developer may have spent several hours in meetings. Other than that, urgent support requests could also have consumed additional time.

The actual development effort could be much lower than three full working days. This distinction matters during estimation.

Teams should record focused development time separately from interruptions. They should also track waiting periods caused by blocked tasks. A more appropriate approach would be to use active development hours to estimate similar future work. They should also account for recurring interruptions when calculating sprint capacity.

For example, a task may regularly wait for security approval. Adding more development hours to its estimate won't resolve that delay.

Instead, the team should recognize the dependency and consider its effect on delivery.

If managers are able to separate these activities, it will help create better estimates without confusing effort with elapsed time.

A precise estimate can create false confidence when a task involves substantial uncertainty.

Suppose similar tasks previously required between 12 and 20 hours. If you estimate the next task at exactly 14 hours, it will overlook important differences.

A range of 12–20 hours provides a clearer picture of the possible effort. Developers can then discuss what might push the task toward either end of the range.

For example, the lower estimate can be directly applied to integration, and the upper estimate might be considered for unfamiliar code or additional testing.

In such scenarios, if the requirements stay unclear, consider a short technical investigation before finalizing the estimate. This can help identify dependencies and reduce uncertainty.

However, estimate ranges should still support practical planning decisions. Teams need to determine how much capacity to reserve for the work.

Choose a planning assumption that reflects the task's risk and available capacity.

As developers learn more about the task, they can refine the estimate.

Historical time data should affect how much work a team accepts during sprint planning. Better estimates offer little value if teams continue making unrealistic commitments.

If several sprints end with unfinished tasks, it is best that you first review why the team missed its commitments in the first place. Once you have identified the causes, use those findings to reduce the next sprint's scope.

Teams should prioritize work that supports the sprint goal. Moreover, avoid adding extra tasks just because they appear manageable during planning.

Production incidents and urgent requests can disrupt planned development. To get more reliable estimations, review how much time these activities consumed in previous sprints. Then reserve an appropriate amount of capacity.

Historical estimates become less reliable in case unfamiliar technology or additional dependencies are involved. It is best to break complex tasks into smaller components where possible. This helps developers assess the work more accurately.

Nominal capacity represents scheduled working hours, whereas actual capacity reflects the time available after accounting for other commitments.

The Scrum Guide identifies previous performance and upcoming capacity as important inputs to sprint forecasting.

When you are selecting backlog items, consider both. Additionally, avoid committing every available hour or deducting the same interruption twice.

Historical time data is useful for sprint planning. Most development teams already use project management tools that include basic time tracking features.

However, these tools don't always break down how developers spend their working hours. For clearer time data, a feature-specific time tracker is more useful because it provides more details about tasks and workloads.

TimeBee is one such developer time tracking tool I've used. It tracks working hours and time spent on projects and tasks. It also provides attendance records and workforce activity reports.

With this data, teams can review how much time previous tasks required. For example, developers can record hours spent building a feature. They can also track debugging and revisions separately. Later, managers can compare these records with the original estimates. This helps them plan similar work more accurately.

TimeBee also tracks website and application activity. These reports can help teams understand how working hours are distributed.

However, not all activities reflect actual development effort. Sometimes a developer just spends time reviewing documentation or discussing technical problems. Such work may not always appear in TimeBee as active computer use.

Apart from that, attendance, leave, and overtime records from TimeBee can help managers calculate sprint capacity. They can review developer availability before deciding how much work to include in the next sprint.

One thing to remember is that developer time tracking should help teams understand workload patterns. It shouldn't be used to monitor every minute of a developer's day. Management should be careful that the aim is to improve estimates and make more realistic sprint commitments.

Sprint retrospectives should include a review of how the planning is going. If you have made a plan, it doesn’t mean you cannot make changes to it. During sprints, keep comparing original estimates with actual task hours. Track your unfinished work for recurring delays.

Ask developers what caused significant differences. Some tasks may have required additional testing. Others may have been delayed by dependencies. Select one specific improvement for the next sprint.

Not every estimation difference indicates a planning failure. Unexpected technical challenges are part of software development.

The objective is to improve forecasts over time rather than eliminate every variance.

Historical time data provides a more stable foundation for sprint planning. Developer time tracking helps teams understand what is actually happening within the sprint rather than creating unnecessary pressure for them.

Other than that, the data also reveals how interruptions affect available development hours.

However, time data should guide planning rather than dictate it.

1. What is the difference between sprint velocity and time-based capacity planning?

Sprint velocity measures how much work a team has completed during a sprint. It is usually measured in story points. Contrary to this, time-based capacity planning calculates the hours available for planned work. It accounts for meetings and other responsibilities.

Velocity helps predict future delivery based on past performance. Capacity planning shows whether the team has enough time to meet its commitments.

2. Which time tracking metrics are most useful for improving Agile sprint forecasts? The most useful metrics include estimated versus actual hours, task completion time, and unplanned work.

Bug-fix time, rework, and blocked tasks also provide valuable insights. Teams should also review available capacity across previous sprints.

These metrics help sort out recurring delays and estimation errors. They allow teams to forecast workloads more accurately and make realistic sprint commitments.

**3. How can development teams improve estimates when historical time tracking data is incomplete? **

Teams can start with available task records and completed sprint backlogs. Developer feedback can help fill information gaps. Similar tasks from previous projects may also provide useful estimates.

When data is limited, teams should use estimate ranges instead of fixed hours. Consistent time tracking in future sprints will gradually provide more reliable planning data.

4. How can time tracking data help balance workloads across developers during sprint planning?

Time tracking data shows how much time developers spend on assigned tasks and other responsibilities. Managers can review these records to identify uneven workloads. They can then distribute upcoming tasks based on available capacity.

Technical expertise and task complexity should also influence assignments. This helps prevent over commitment and supports a more balanced workload across the development team.

#10472. China’s Air Force builds drone that shoots painkiller darts at wounded soldiers

This won't magically bring units to 100% health, but it should increase survival rates for wounded soldiers in the frontlines.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

A Chinese military medical school has just developed a UAV that could remotely administer medicine, helping treat injured soldiers on the front lines without putting medical personnel at risk. According to Chinese mouthpiece Global Times, the Air Force Medical University built the first casualty-search drone for the People’s Liberation Army (PLA) that fires darts loaded with painkillers to help ease the pain of wounded personnel.

“We have developed the military’s first casualty-search drone and have also made some breakthroughs in treatment. For example, we can use drones to inject medication into wounded personnel from a considerable distance,” Air Force Medical University representative Wang Jianqi told the publication. “Wherever our drone can fly, we can deliver life-saving emergency medication into a wounded person’s muscle, buying valuable time for treatment.”

Russia’s invasion of Ukraine saw the widespread use of drones on the frontline, primarily for search-and-destroy and reconnaissance missions. However, the four-year escalation of the 12-year war has seen the creative implementations of these units — from jam-proof fiber optic drones to the use of heavy-lift hexacopters for medical evacuation. Now, drones-as-first-responders are being developed for civilian use, including firefighting drones, camera response drones, and even “Campus Guardian Angel” drones designed to neutralize school shooters.

However, China's air-ground treatment drone remains in the experimental stage, says Wang. After all, you can’t just administer medication without assessing the patient’s vital signs and their injuries. It’s also difficult or even impossible to load an incapacitated soldier without external assistance. Still, the deployment of medical UAVs would help with battlefield medicine, which has developed the concept of the “golden hour,” which suggests that wounded personnel treated within the first hour have the greatest chance of survival.

Although battlefield medicine was in use since the 1800s, especially during Napoleon’s invasion of Russia and the American Civil War, helicopter medevacs were first deployed during World War 2 in Burma and the Philippines. The Korean War saw the introduction of MASH — Mobile Army Surgical Hospital — units that brought forward treatment units closer to the frontline.

This, combined with the widespread use of UH-1 Hueys during the Vietnam War to evacuate wounded soldiers from the front lines, sometimes while under enemy fire, has greatly increased the survival rate among wounded soldiers. Today, helicopter and ground medevacs are common among military forces, with drone treatment and evacuation now being experimented with in both the frontlines of Ukraine and military medical universities in China.

#10469. Wyze’s new door lock has speedy fingerprint recognition and 12-month battery life

Wyze is today launching its new “Lever Lock,” a smart lock for your front door that has a year of battery life and fast fingerprint recognition, all for an affordable price tag.

Available starting today, the Wyze Level Lock is not your typical deadbolt smart lock. Rather, this is a smart lock designed for your standard single-bore latch, meaning it’s available for doors that other smart locks aren’t compatible with. You can still have a deadbolt, but you’ll need enough space for both (per Wyze’s guidelines below).

The Lever Lock has a PIN entry and a fingerprint sensor, the latter of which Wyze says recognizes your fingerprint in about half a second. It uses local storage for biometric data and works to improve that data with each successful unlock.

The PIN has a clever feature (though it’s presented in somewhat poor taste) that lets you input random digits before or after your actual code to prevent onlookers from being able to see your code. There’s also a physical key backup as well as a USB-C input on the outside in the case that the battery dies – Wyze says it lasts about 12 months on a charge using eight AA batteries.

The Wyze Lever Lock is available today for $99.98 and shipping immediately.

#10466. Lawyer’s eight-V100 AI server moves DeepSeek from 2-bit to near-NVFP4 precision

Join for quick access. Enter your email below and we'll send confirmation plus sign you up to our newsletter.

Get started with free access to reviews, badges and discussions.

The upgrade costs speed: code output falls to 150–180 tokens per second, from 160–215.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

A lawyer with a private AI server built on eight used Nvidia V100 GPUs returned with an update, moving about 80% of its work up from 2-bit to 4-bit NVFP4 precision and bringing in 1TB of RAM and an RTX 3090. The server uses DeepSeek V4.1 Flash with a legal harness, the workflows and checks he wraps around the model, for his law practice. The cost? Performance, with code output dropping from 160–215 to 150–180 tokens per second, although the crashes that hit the new build are gone. The culprit behind the crashes, originally blamed on PCIe, was likely a bad PSU.

Once a random hardware nerd lawyer on Reddit, the lawyer's responses to the original breaking story illuminated his identity. He’s a trial lawyer in Columbia, South Carolina. ProprietaryLegalAI commented on our first story three times, linking to a code repository, site, and LinkedIn that identify him as Nick Mermiges, founder of PLI Labs and proprietarylegal.ai. His other firm, PLI, is a technology consultancy, not a law firm, and now lists private, on-prem LLM serving as a service.

He built the software side with AI, specifically Claude Code, which wrote the kernels and ran the benchmarks with his direction. The Opus 5.5 and Fable 5.2 models handled the optimizations, custom kernels, and re-quantizing; he noted he was surprised at how well Fable built custom Volta kernels, and how it re-quantizes the experts his legal harness triggers. From about February to July, he built a legal harness around Claude that worked, while his attempts at a local-model version failed, but then DeepSeek V4.1 Flash arrived, and he was able to port it successfully, even if he says it’s not as good as frontier.

He reaffirms that “the documents never leave the office” and upgraded the system from 256GB to 1TB of RAM and tossed in a newer GPU, even as the V100s stay on “borrowed time” with R580, the last Nvidia driver branch to support them. The project’s intentions, as listed last time, suggested he wanted the system to be able to offload more of his workload, so this and the precision change are a reasonable upgrade. His previously stated goal of wanting the system to handle more than one request at a time has also been met.

The original “Volta sPLIt” build we covered has transformed into the “Volta Max uPLIft,” still with the clever lettering as his company’s initials are PLI — Proprietary Legal Intelligence. He now claims “near-NVFP4 precision,” having described NVFP4 on our forum earlier this week as “essentially full precision.” NVFP4 is Nvidia’s 4-bit floating-point format, and Nvidia publishes its own NVFP4 version of DeepSeek V4.1 Flash; the project’s README says that version matches the precision of the model’s official weights at 4.25 bits per weight.

DeepSeek V4.1 Flash is built from “experts,” the parts of the model that do most of the work for each word. This model didn’t fit at that precision before, as 384 routed experts, in each of 40 layers, counts to 15,360 in all. At official precision, this comes out to about 269 GiB, by his estimate, and eight 32GB V100s have 256 GiB in total before anything else is loaded. With the 2-bit build, the weights were 248.8GB; the new build’s Hugging Face package is about 556GB.

The workaround is to keep every expert on the cards at 2-bit and swap the most-used ones up until the cards are full, which in this case came out to about 38.7%, or 5,949 experts, at precisely NVFP4. “Most-used,” in this context, refers to a count from a mix of four kinds of text, which are weighted equally, one of which is his own legal harness traffic.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

The payoff is that those experts do most of the work. In testing with one request at a time, between 80% and 86% of decode routes, the calls to an expert made while the model writes, land on the NVFP4 experts, lowest for general text and highest for source code. The prediction for the two-request setup he runs is 78% to 85%. The remaining 15% to 22% of calls would still go to a 2-bit expert.

Measured against Nvidia’s own version of the model as the reference, he was able to get good results. Same next token, or how often a build picks the same next token as the reference, went up to 95.1% from 92.2%. For KL divergence, which measures how far a build’s probabilities are from the reference’s, it went down to 0.079 nats from 0.196. In measuring multiple-choice answers that change, across 513 questions from a standard multiple-choice knowledge test, it went from 79 different to just 8.

This does not measure accuracy — which was about the same across all three cases — but fidelity. Those tests ran on the build’s first release, but not on the new two-request setup he now runs.

The result of the changes: higher fidelity but at a lower level of performance. Code output is 3% to 30% slower, depending on the prompt length, and legal writing is 9% to 23% slower. Before the build, he hoped to maintain about 80 tokens per second on legal and prose; the bench came in at 86 to 106 for legal and 78 to 86 for prose. The upside is that running two requests at once raises total output to 159.0 tokens per second, versus 118.9 with a sole request. The first token also arrives sooner at every prompt length, 108.4 seconds versus 118.7 seconds at 256K tokens.

Each request needs a “slot,” though, and this comes at a cost. Every slot keeps its own state on the cards, which leaves room for fewer NVFP4 experts: 6,215, 5,949, 5,663 for one, two, and three slots, respectively. This means each added slot may push another one to two points of the calls back to 2-bit, which could reduce output quality.

Legal writing (tokens/s), 2-bit / near-NVFP4

Builder’s measurements, Nov. 5 (2-bit) and Nov. 12 (near-NVFP4); greedy decoding, thinking off, one request at a time.

The move to more RAM came this week with eight 128GB modules at 2,933 MT/s, originally eight 32GB modules that had to be run at DDR4-2666 due to ECC errors. After the RAM upgrade, the host reports two NUMA nodes, and two boots hung at the first transfer between the boards, so he reordered the cards in software. The GPUs remain capped at 1,522 MHz, as before.

As for the PSU, he took the common approach of chasing PCIe slots, cards, and cables to narrow down the cause, finally concluding it was “probably one bad PSU.” He also added more fans, but with no temperature changes, this only indicated that airflow was not the problem. He changed the power delivery, and nine of nine long chats read clean afterward. Also, don’t judge his cable management skill; any “advice [there] is welcome and will be ignored,” which is the only comment to make in that situation.

He also already has two DGX Sparks running the same model at 1M context for agent work, and three or four sessions at once, and he isn’t stopping there. Now it comes down to scaling. Eight more V100s and a second server, with 512GB of 3,200 MT/s ECC RAM, are waiting in the wings. The second box would run another high-precision DeepSeek, used as an extra agent or to run tasks in parallel. The plan is also to move DeepSeek’s small draft model off the V100s and onto the RTX 3090, which hasn’t happened yet.

#10462. How to Improve Email Deliverability Rates

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

You wrote a good email. You hit send. Then half your list never saw it, because it went straight to spam.

That is a deliverability problem, and it costs more than most teams think. Every email that misses the inbox is a lead you paid for and never reached.

The good part is that deliverability is not luck. Mailbox providers like Gmail, Yahoo and Outlook judge you on a short list of things: who you are, who you send to, and how people react. Fix those and your inbox placement goes up.

This guide covers 10 email deliverability best practices in the order you should fix them, starting with SPF and ending with sender reputation.

Email deliverability is the share of your emails that land in the inbox instead of the spam folder or nowhere at all.

People often mix it up with delivery rate. They are not the same thing.

You can have a 99% delivery rate and still have most of your emails sitting in spam. That is why your email tool can say "delivered" while your open rate keeps dropping.

The big mailbox providers stopped treating authentication as optional.

Microsoft followed on May 5, 2025. If you send more than 5,000 emails a day to Outlook.com, Hotmail or Live addresses, you need all three records in place or your mail goes to junk.

Send less than 5,000 a day? Follow the rules anyway. The same checks decide inbox placement for small senders too, just without the public warning.

SPF (Sender Policy Framework) is a TXT record in your DNS. It lists the servers allowed to send email from your domain.

When your email arrives, the receiving server checks that list. If the sending server is not on it, the email looks suspicious.

DKIM (DomainKeys Identified Mail) adds a digital signature to each email. The receiving server uses a public key in your DNS to check two things: the email came from you, and nobody changed it on the way.

Your email provider gives you the DKIM record. You paste it into your DNS and switch signing on.

DKIM also survives forwarding, which SPF does not. If you can only get one of the two perfect, get DKIM right.

DMARC ties SPF and DKIM together. It tells mailbox providers what to do when an email fails both checks, and it sends you reports on who is sending mail under your domain name.

p=none means "take no action, just send me reports." It is the minimum Gmail, Yahoo and Microsoft accept from bulk senders.

Don't stop there. Read the reports for a few weeks, fix any real sender that is failing, then move up:

One more thing: DMARC needs alignment. The domain in your From address has to match the domain used in SPF or DKIM. Plenty of senders have all three records and still fail DMARC because their email tool signs with its own domain.

If you'd rather not handle this in-house, you can also hire a B2B cold email marketing agency such as IntentSignal, Martal or Belkins. A good agency will set up the domains, mailboxes, warm-up and SPF, DKIM and DMARC records for you, and keep an eye on deliverability while the campaigns run.

A new domain has no history. If it sends 10,000 emails on day one, it looks exactly like a spammer who just bought a domain.

Warm-up means starting small and growing a little each day, so mailbox providers can watch how people react to you.

Send to your most active contacts first. Their opens and replies are what build trust early. If bounces or complaints jump, hold at that volume until they settle.

And if you run cold outreach, do it from a separate domain or subdomain. That way a bad campaign can't hurt the domain your invoices and password resets come from.

A bigger list is not a better list. Dead addresses and people who stopped caring pull your numbers down.

Never buy a list. Bought lists are full of spam traps, which are addresses that exist only to catch senders who email people without permission. Hitting a few of them can get your domain blocklisted.

Yes, you lose some sign-ups. What you keep is real people with working inboxes who actually want your emails. That means fewer bounces, fewer complaints and better opens from day one.

It also keeps bots and fake addresses out of your forms, which matters a lot if your sign-up form is public.

Most email platforms add these for you. Check that yours does.

These two numbers tell you the most about where you stand.

The complaint figures come from Gmail's own rules. The bounce figures are the working limits most email platforms use.

A 0.3% complaint rate is only 3 complaints per 1,000 emails. It does not take much to cross that line.

If complaints are climbing, ask three questions:

Most complaint problems come from one of those three, not from the email itself.

Spam filters got smarter. A single word like "free" won't sink you anymore. Patterns will.

Replies help a lot. When someone replies, the mailbox provider sees a real conversation. Ask a question in your welcome email and you'll get some.

Sender reputation is the score mailbox providers give your domain and IP. Everything above feeds into it. A good score means inbox. A bad one means spam, no matter how good the email is.

You can't see the exact score, but these free tools get you close:

Check them weekly, and right after any big campaign.

A few habits protect your reputation over time:

Aim for 95% or higher inbox placement. Below 90% means something needs fixing, usually authentication or list quality.

The common causes are missing or broken SPF, DKIM or DMARC records, a high complaint rate, an old list with lots of dead addresses, or a new domain that sent too much too soon. Check authentication first because it is the fastest to fix.

DNS fixes like SPF and DKIM take effect within a day or two. Reputation is slower. Expect two to six weeks of steady, clean sending before inbox placement recovers.

Yes. The 5,000 mark is where Gmail, Yahoo and Microsoft start enforcing openly. Small senders are still judged on the same signals, and Gmail asks every sender for at least SPF or DKIM.

Only if you send enough to keep it warm, roughly 100,000 emails a month or more. Below that, a good shared IP from a trusted provider usually performs better.

Sender reputation covers both your IP address and your domain. Domain reputation is the part tied to your domain name, and it follows you even if you switch email providers. Gmail leans on domain reputation more than IP.

If you only have an hour this week, spend it on SPF, DKIM and DMARC. Nothing else works until those pass.

After that, clean your list and keep an eye on complaints. Send emails people asked for, to people who still want them, from a domain that proves who it is. Do that for a few weeks and your emails will land in the inbox again.

#10457. Weeks after shipment was swiped by China, Pentagon ‘struggles to account for’ $31 billion in missing F-35 aircraft parts

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

Unlock instant access to exclusive member features.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

The Government Accountability Office says record-keeping, inventory monitoring, and staff training need to be improved.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

The Pentagon is struggling to track an astonishing 26 million F-35 warfighter parts, worth an estimated $31 billion, according to analysis published by the Government Accountability Office (GAO) this week. In its report — published just weeks after China purportedly swiped a classified shipment of F-35 parts diverted through Hong Kong — the GAO criticized the current inventory system as “outdated, inaccurate, and incomplete.” Possible solutions were also provided by the GAO; however, the implications are that the most advanced and costly fighter jets in U.S. history may not be ready and reliable due to inventory management deficiencies.

Inventory problems have been a long-standing feature of the deployment of the F-35, notes the source. The F-35 program is estimated to cost $1.9 trillion over its lifecycle. It is the most costly weapon system in history, says the GAO. But the Department of Defense (DOD) has “long-standing and ongoing deficiencies related to the accountability and financial reporting of F-35 parts,” asserts the watchdog.

Chief among the issues that need to be addressed is the headlining massive unmonitored pool of roughly 26 million spare parts. These are spread among 700 government and contractor facilities worldwide, but the technology for managing massive inventories has existed for decades.

We might have seen a telling symptom of the inadequate F-35 parts tracking system back in September. A communications and/or accountability breakdown resulted in a UPS worker routing top-secret parts through Hong Kong to save time. They were allegedly swiped by China after an employee is thought to have missed an email warning not to divert the parts.

Frustratingly, this is the third report since 2019 from the GAO, and the latest published report complains that the DOD has “made minimal progress” in implementing past recommendations. For example, in a 2022 report, periodic physical inventories of F-35 parts were recommended but have not been implemented yet, notes Stars and Stripes. There is also no reliable procedure for verifying data from prime contractors, which can be “inaccurate and incomplete.”

Currently, it is believed that there are over 1 million parts, worth more than $85 million, that are lost or missing. Moreover, about 17,000 parts that have been sent in for repair, worth as much as $42.5 billion, aren’t tracked.

Stars and Stripes mentions that the Pentagon is working on “a live connection” for the F-35 Joint Program Office (JPO), bridging its inventory software and that of prime contractors with F-35 parts. The JPO asserts that it is now modernizing its infrastructure with direct real-time interfaces. “This integration directly addresses previous system gaps to ensure seamless accountability of F-35 spare parts,” said a JPO spokesperson, quoted by Breaking Defense.

#10453. How to Use IP Geolocation to Stop Bots and Fraud (Without Hurting UX)

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Several methods can help you deal with online threats and strengthen security. All are effective, but each has a trade-off. For example, the harder you make it for bots to affect your website’s performance, the more likely you are to frustrate real customers.

Those of you who browse online daily know that credential stuffing, fake signups & payment fraud keep growing. To prevent fraud, businesses install CAPTCHAs & hard blocks that treat bots and legitimate users the same, diverting them to your competitors.

IP geolocation maps an IP address to a physical location & network details, letting you spot suspicious traffic before it reaches sensitive actions. Used well, it’s an invisible, low-friction risk signal. In this guide, you will learn how to flag high-risk activity and apply soft geo-fencing with step-up authentication.

Today, an IP lookup returns far more than a country & city. It can also reveal several other parameters, like

Each of these fields acts as a clue telling who is really behind a request.

This data powers anomaly detection. You can start by building a baseline for normal behavior for each user with aspects like

Then, you can watch for deviations from that pattern. An attacker won’t look like the account’s real owner, so you'll catch them.

Geolocation makes several common attack patterns easy to spot:

The first is traffic bursts from data center ASNs. These are bots, as real customers don’t browse from cloud servers

Many accounts created from one IP or subnet. This is a clear sign of fake signups

Impossible travel, such as logins from two distant locations within minutes

Timezone vs. IP mismatch. A device’s clock disagrees with its apparent location.

Note: Geolocation is just a signal, not a verdict. VPNs, travel, & mobile carriers can all create false alarms, so it works best when combined with other signals. That’s exactly why a proportional, soft response beats a hard block.

In short, IP geolocation turns a bare IP address into context, helping you separate real users from automated or fraudulent traffic.

Not every IP geolocation anomaly deserves the same response. The simplest way to stay consistent is to define a clear risk-signal framework.

That can be a short list of checks. Each check can have a weight that reflects how strongly it suggests fraud.

With risk scoring, you can turn these checks into a decision. You just have to assign each signal a numeric weight.

For example, medium can be 10 & 30 can be high.

Now, you need to add up every signal that comes up & map the total to thresholds like

A single medium signal passes quietly, but several can trigger action.

If you treat this as an automatic block, it would punish legitimate customers. Instead, let it raise the score & you take action only if it stacks with other signals, like a hosting IP or a new device.

Now that you know how to quantify the risk, here is how you can protect yourself without damaging UX.

Before soft, let us learn about hard blocking. This is where you completely deny or permanently ban traffic from specific geographic regions.

Now, hard blocking by country may look effective, but it can backfire quickly. Here is how.

In the worst case, determined bot operators simply rotate to IPs in allowed regions, so the only people you stop are your real customers.

This is where the balance between security and user experience becomes critical. As the digital strategist and Founder at LawRank, Mariano Rodriguez emphasizes, "Your website's security should be a silent guardian, not a roadblock. If your fraud prevention creates friction, you aren't just stopping bots—you're turning away real clients. True digital trust is built when backend protections are seamless and invisible to the legitimate user."

Now, with soft geo-fencing, you can respond to risk proportionally instead of making a simple allow-or-deny call. Instead of totally banning the users, you adjust the level of friction to match the risk score from the previous section:

**Note: **For soft geo-fencing, passkeys are safest because they are phishing-resistant. Only a legitimate user can take the biometric tap and pass.

Before we start, let’s understand Express.js middleware. An Express.js middleware for Geolocation Risk Scores is a custom or 3rd party function that intercepts the incoming HTTP requests, extracts the client's IP address, fetches a security risk profile based on their geographic location & attaches that data to the request object (req) before it reaches your route handlers.

Here is how you can use it for fraud prevention and bot mitigation:

Note: You can make it stronger by adding velocity checks (too many attempts from one IP or account) & device fingerprints.

From a broader angle, IP geolocation isn't the final verdict. It is just a signal. But with this signal, you can score each request, respond in proportion to the risk & save hard blocks for clear abuse. This increases the friction for bots and keeps the flow easy for legitimate users.

#10451. Contractor turns a Milwaukee Hackzall into a portable Ethernet untwister after facing job requiring 50,000 custom Cat6 cables

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

A contractor faced a project that required them to create 50,000 custom Cat6 cables from scratch, requiring 100,000 Cat6 terminations. The contractor, who goes by Analytical Horsepower on YouTube, had used a large, rare tool to streamline the job, but afterward he decided to build his own portable tool by modifying a Milwaukee Hackzall, cutting the time to straighten the twisted pairs to under two seconds.

His first solution to the problem was to buy a “Wire Straightening Machine” from Alibaba for $500. It does the job well enough, but it’s an industrial machine designed for tabletop use, requiring an AC power source for the power brick and compressed air to run. The concept is that it clamps the wire between two rubber grips using air pressure, and then a linkage converts the rotary motion of the motor into a linear one, allowing the grip to untwist the cable. Since he wanted to use the machine in the field, he needed to make the thing portable — so he disassembled the entire thing, replaced the DC power brick with a 20V DeWalt battery with an adapter, added a push-button switch for power control, and a portable bike pump to deliver compressed air on the go.

It worked well at first, but he soon discovered that it was too bulky to use in the tight and hard-to-reach places where Ethernet cables sometimes terminate, like when you’re installing this heavy-duty outdoor Wi-Fi 7 access point. So, he studied the concept — it involved two flat, grippy surfaces grabbing the twisted cables and then moving back and forth. The back-and-forth motion of the surfaces causes the cables to untwist, with the clamping force ensuring that it remains in contact with the cables as they become flatter.

With this, he realized that he did not need such a bulky machine to do the job — he just needed a reciprocating tool that could accept a clamp. He first looked at a reciprocating saw, but he found that it still had the same bulky problem and also called it “not overly precise.” But when he looked at the Milwaukee Hackzall, he found that it offered the perfect balance of portability and precision. More importantly, it came with a detailed data sheet, instruction manual, and parts breakdown that allowed him to accurately 3D print the plastic parts and laser-cut and bend the metal parts he needed to build his machine. He also used a toggle clamp to hold the Cat6 cable in place instead of a pneumatic piston, which further simplified the machine and cut its size and weight.

After a few steps of trial and error, especially in the quality of the grippy materials used for the clamping surfaces, he finally got it to work. He could reliably untwist softer copper-clad aluminum cables in one go in under two seconds. Harder 100% pure copper conductors, sometimes called solid copper or pure bare copper Ethernet cables, must be untwisted pair-by-pair, though, requiring a bit more time, but it’s still undoubtedly much faster and more convenient than doing the job with just your bare hands.

This tool isn’t worth it if you only untwist a few Ethernet cables every month or so, like this telephone-to-gigabit internet conversion job. But if you’re facing a massive job like Analytical Horsepower, that half a minute you save with every cable end could translate to over 830 cumulative hours of labor saved on the jobsite. It’s a great first-generation tool, with its practical use apparent in the YouTube video. Some commenters suggest extending the toggle clamp handle to reach the trigger so activating the tool is easier, while it also looks like a good idea if there’s a way to incorporate the entire system into a crimper.

#10449. Is a gas oven bad for bread?: Why a gas oven may be interfering with your go-to steaming method.

Adapted from King Arthur Baking Company’s Big Book of Bread

One of the most crucial steps for making crusty artisan-style bread comes at the very end: steaming.

Baking bread with steam is critical for achieving expansive, open-crumbed loaves with a golden, crisp crust. If you bake bread in a dry oven, the crust quickly sets, which prevents the dough from expanding to its full potential; this, in turn, leads to loaves with a denser crumb. If you introduce steam at the start of the bake, the crust remains moist and pliable and the loaf can stretch and expand before the crust sets, resulting in a lighter, more open crumb.

What’s more, steam also helps gelatinize starches on the loaf’s exterior, which helps it brown and creates an especially crispy, flavorful, and shiny loaf. (Learn more: A guide to baking bread with steam at home.)

There are many different ways to steam your bread, all of which are designed to introduce moisture to the oven during the first part of baking. The most common method involves setting a large cast-iron skillet below your baking stone or steel when you preheat the oven. After you load your bread into the oven, you immediately pour a cup or so of warm water into the preheated cast-iron skillet and quickly close the door so the steam doesn’t escape.

This method works great, with one exception: It’s not effective in a gas oven.

If you have a gas oven, it’s very hard to add sufficient steam. By design, gas ovens vent out effluence, meaning whatever steam you add is very quickly vented out of the oven chamber. This is particularly true if you steam your bread by pouring hot water into a cast-iron skillet, as outlined above. All the water quickly dissipates, and there’s not enough moisture left to enhance the crust.

But all is not lost. There is a workaround.

If you’re steaming bread in a gas oven, you need to use a preheated covered baker, such as a Dutch oven. Instead of adding an additional water source, the lidded vessel traps the steam that escapes from the loaf as it bakes, becoming a kind of self-steaming oven. Because the steam is sealed in, the gas oven won’t vent it out.

You can use a Dutch oven for a round, boule-shaped loaf, or any other form of covered baker, like the Brod & Taylor Bread Steel for elongated, bâtard-shaped loaves. (There’s also a round version of the Bread Steel available.)

For more guidance on this method, see our previous blogs, Bread baking in a Dutch oven and For crispy, crackle crusts, put a lid on it.

When you load your dough into the oven, slip a few ice cubes between the parchment beneath your loaf and the pan to give the loaf an extra initial burst of steam. Just don’t skip the parchment beneath the dough. Otherwise, the extra water from the melting ice may cause your bread to stick to the pot.

Cover photo and food styling by Martin Philip.

#10447. I’ve waited a long time for the Boox Picco, and I’m not disappointed

At around $100, the Boox Picco offers one of the most portable e-reader experiences you can get. You add MagSafe-like capability with a refreshingly simple UI, and you have a delightful way to read, literally wherever you are.

There is an argument to be made for every single shape and size e-readers come in, and they come in so many different sizes it’s hard to keep track. I love the idea of having a 10-inch display that I can skim, but even if it’s perfect for sitting at home and reading anything from novels to comics, I don’t think it provides a very good travel experience

But even smaller devices like the Boox Palma make you choose between bringing a second device or sticking with your phone. Most often, the phone wins out – especially when you have a flexible approach with a foldable like the Galaxy Z Fold 8 I’ve been using daily.

Unlike the other phone-sized e-readers from Boox, the Picco stands at just 4.15 inches tall. The screen measures at just under that – 3.97 inches. It’s essentially the size of a MagSafe or Qi2 wallet – just small enough to fit on the back of your phone with the included magnetic case.

It’s thin, too. A 5.5mm side profile means it slips anywhere a minimal wallet would without issue. That goes for pockets just as well as crossbody bags; perfect for travel.

And that’s where I think I find so much more joy in using this over what would otherwise be considered a small e-reader, like the Palma. I don’t have a lot of excuses not to carry it around when I’m on the go. I’ll throw it in my Bellroy bag when I’m carrying what I’d considered my minimal EDC. It slides into any internal pocket, and it weighs next to nothing.

The back of the Boox Picco isn’t magnetic, but the included case is. If you want the thinnest possible footprint, forego the case. But adding it lets you do one of the coolest – and slightly copium-inducing – things with your phone: attach the e-ink device directly to your OLED-laden phone.

It does not work well with an unconventional device like my Galaxy Z Fold 8, but if you have nearly any other device like the Pixel 11, you should get a rather perfect fit. The Picco squares up to the back of any slab phone with Qi2 magnets, whether internal or via a case.

But, why? Why slap another device and add weight/thickness to your phone when it’s perfectly capable of displaying e-books?

Because it’s cool! That’s the driving reason. You can make the argument that it doubles your phone’s usability or that it gives you an easy place to store the tiny e-reader. The truth is that it does add extra thickness to your phone, and using your phone with the Picco piggybacking along can be uncomfortable, since your fingers now rest on another display and not a case or phone backing.

You can get used to it, and, as unnecessary as it may seem, it’s a fun way to carry around a much more eye-friendly screen.

The Picco diverts from other Boox devices in another way. It doesn’t run Android, which means you have no access to third-party apps like Google Play Books, Amazon Kindle, or any other store/library. Instead, the Picco uses a companion app to transfer EPUBs from your phone to the tiny e-reader.

I initially opposed this idea when I got a sneak peek during IFA in Berlin this year. But after using it for a few weeks, I’ll admit I’ve come around. But that all comes down to how you consume your media.

I try to source EPUBs no matter what device I use. On Android-powered e-readers, that means downloading Google Play Books and reading from there; I have my EPUBs uploaded to my own Google Play Books library, so they’re available universally.

On the Picco, my approach hasn’t changed much. I take those same EPUBs I have in storage and transfer them from the companion app.

I can understand how the lack of third-party libraries would make the Boox Picco moot. Still, I’ve been a proponent of buying digital books I can store in my own drives, so I can read them wherever, whether that’s the Google Play Books library or from a device like the Picco.

All of that is a roundabout way of saying the Picco, though it doesn’t let you use apps to read, makes it easy to get digital copies loaded and ready to flip through.

It uses a handshake connection that requires you to open the companion app on your phone, hit connect, and do the same on the Picco. Once connected, transferring EPUB files is as easy as selecting them from your phone’s physical or cloud storage and queueing them up. The process takes seconds, and the only issue I’ve had was solved by a force stop and trying again.

The homescreen is simply a carousel of your library, with a delightful little progress bar under each title as they’re sorted. You can access and manage the bookshelf from that page, as well as the minimal “apps” and settings. I appreciate the focus app and reading stats, but the countdown and to-do options require using the companion app. I don’t see the point of those.

It doesn’t even have a control panel. Swiping down from the top surfaces screen brightness and color controls. Even though this little guy is just over $100, it still has a front light controlled by a physical switch on the side. Compare that to e-readers at much higher price points without that game-changing feature.

The buttons opposite don’t control volume. Instead, they’re there for page turning.

It’s just simple and well-designed. Features are there to make reading a better experience, down to granular text controls for your digital books. It does exactly what it needs to do – nothing more, nothing less.

As far as battery goes, it meets my expectations. It’s not massive, so the battery is small in comparison to a smartphone. It still holds its own easily, with an easy week of use if you’re reading for an hour or two a day. Beyond that, it takes little time to charge and does great on idle, which is perfect for such a passive device.

The Picco will hit home for you if you want an ultra-portable option for your e-books. The same goes for those who don’t mind the smaller screen and lack of Android or third-party app support.

I get genuine joy every time I pick it up to read where I left off, and I think that’s because it’s so simple and nothing about it feels bloated, even the companion app, which is also straight to the point.

I think it’s been a while since a device has offered that experience for me. Adding the form factor, optional magnetic attachment for your phone, and price tag just gets me to that conclusion sooner.

The Boox Picco is the most adorable e-reader I’ve used, and it’s $109 on Amazon.

#10444. Researchers allege DeepSeek, Kimi, and other Chinese AI models were trained using banned Nvidia Blackwell GPUs

Reviews, benchmarks, and updates on current GPUs.

Join for quick access. Enter your email below and we'll send confirmation plus sign you up to our newsletter.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

Unlock instant access to exclusive member features.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

A clever yet simple way to establish correlation.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

The topic of U.S. trade controls precluding the export of Nvidia and AMD high-end AI accelerator chips to China has taken a few twists lately. Jensen Huang's affairs aside, it's an open secret in the industry that Nvidia's Blackwell silicon finds its way into China, whether physically or virtually. UnemploymentLabs, an independent research outfit, has come up with strong evidence that those chips were used for training open-weight Chinese AI models, including DeepSeek V4, Kimi K3.1, MiniMax-M3, and Ling-3.1-Flash.

The scientists are careful to hedge their findings, noting that while their math checks out, it does not reveal where the chips were, how they were accessed, or prove that export controls were broken. As far as we can tell, it's currently not illegal for Chinese companies to rent Blackwell hardware abroad, and even though Nvidia's grip is tightening, U.S. companies aren't strictly prohibited from doing so, as evidenced by the recent Tencent-Oracle deal.

This identification method is limited to models with open weights, and depends on both hardware and software stacks used for model training — and yet it's both clever and simple, like the Egg of Columbus.

Let's establish some base facts. First, different AI accelerator families have unique counts of shader modules (SMs), marginally analogous to CPU cores — Nvidia's Hopper chips have 132, Blackwell B series have 148, and Blackwell GBs come with 152. Second, while there's more than one software stack for training AI models, PyTorch and its CUDA-aware friends are by far the most widely used on the green team's hardware.

The first step for training a model is initializing its weights, or how strongly the model considers each individual input. In modern LLM architectures, their initialization goes through a randomization step in which their values are adjusted plus/minus pseudo-randomly, using cuRAND — as in, randomly, but a seed starting value results in the same adjustment figures every time.

As you'd expect, doing this for an entire model is a lot of computational work, so this process is spread out across all the SMs, further subdivided into threads. For large weight sets, the thread count per SM is 2,048 for Blackwell and Hopper silicon, and it's surely carefully chosen and optimized for.

The researchers repeated a facsimile of the initialization process using the same chips, software, and thread counts. Using relatively simple statistical analysis, they spotted that their final statistical distribution of weight values precisely matched that of DeepSeek, Kimi, et al. As a test, they changed the SM and thread counts, and predictably, the correlation completely broke down.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

This let them pick out each family of Blackwell/Hopper chip used for each model, and eventually they also figured out an approximate count of cards used. They cannot, however, pinpoint which exact variation produced the weights, as this analysis is linked to initialization layout counts alone, thus SM count and usage pattern — not to VRAM capacity, memory bandwidth, or other characteristics that distinguish individual cards.

What about AMD cards like the MI300X, or other silicon with a high enough SM count, using the same software stack? The scientists say that it's not impossible that an AMD card could be set up to show up as two groups of 152 or 148 shaders, although we imagine there'd be little reason to do so. Nevertheless, for DeepSeek V4, they also ruled out that possibility by analyzing the actual weight values themselves, and found that AMD cards still didn't produce the same weight value pattern.
