#10397. The Lenovo Legion Tab Gen 5 would be my perfect Steam tablet if it didn’t cost $900

For every Android-powered slate I’ve used, only Nvidia’s Shield Tablet really stands out in my memory. Part of the company’s occasional (and all-but-dead) dip into hardware products, the Shield Tablet — and its revitalized K1 follow-up, which I also owned through a recall program on the original — carried me through the back half of college, frequently keeping my Chromebook in my backpack in lieu of a mini Bluetooth keyboard or the Shield Tablet’s built-in stylus. Its front-firing speakers made it great for movie-watching, and while I’m not much of a mobile gamer, I did find it to be a great device for emulation, especially when paired with an external controller.

Even if Nvidia hadn’t abandoned the hardware game at the end of the last decade, it’s found new and much more destructive ways to make piles of cash at the expense of your easy access to RAM. And considering the market for Android tablets in general, it’s not all too surprising to see very few companies trying to make headway into offering a miniature, gaming-focused tablet that can easily be thrown in a backpack. Lenovo, however, has managed to fill its spot quite well. 9to5Google’s own Ben Schoon found himself smitten with the Gen 3 model nearly two years ago, and now, its Gen 5 successor has found its way into my nostalgic hands.

But expectations around mobile experiences have come a long way since 2016. Whether the Legion Tab Gen 5 can win over your heart really depends on your patience with some old-school Android jank — and whether your wallet can withstand an exceptionally-high asking price. Still, with recent advancements in PC emulation, it’s hard to say Lenovo isn’t onto something.

Considering the decade between their respective releases, I’m actually quite surprised just how much this tablet reminds me of the original Shield Tablet. Its 8.8-inch 16:10 LCD panel combined with a boxy matte-black design and a near-identical weight meant that I immediately felt at home holding this thing in my hands. You lose out on a couple of clutch features — most notably the built-in stylus — but largely speaking, this feels like the correct size and shape for a miniature gaming tablet.

Still, it’s not all perfect. Take, for example, the completely unnecessary camera bump that prevents this slate from properly laying flat on a desk. No one is buying this thing to use the camera, and judging by my very limited experience trying it out, I’m fairly confident whatever potato sensor is shoved in here would fit fine flush with the chassis. I’m pretty fed up with OEMs leaning on camera bumps to make any device look or feel more premium than it actually is, and that goes double quadruple for tablets like this.

Just like its predecessor, this thing still picks up smudges and fingerprints at an unreasonable level, its matte-black finish unable to hide anything. 360 grams is also a little heavier than I’d expect for a slate of this size, though to be fair, it’s much more noticeable in portrait than landscape. Holding the tablet with two hands to watch a movie or make my way through another round of Balatro made its top-heaviness much less noticeable.

This display is sharp and fast — with one minor caveat — and reasonably bright in all circumstances except direct sunlight thanks to its 600 nits of brightness (and 800 in HBM). Really, it’s the LCD of it all that gets me here; this thing is nearly $1,000, and the lack of OLED alone makes it difficult to recommend at this price. It’s not an outright bad panel, but I found its contrast levels to be pretty lacking, with most blacks only ever showing up as dark grays on-screen.

Oh, and that caveat: On two different occasions, I caught the tablet stuck in 60Hz mode, regardless of what refresh rate option I selected in settings. Switching back and forth between modes a few times eventually unstuck it, but it’s definitely a bug worth being aware of.

I think I’d feel a lot better about recommending this to someone looking for a content consumption device if this screen looked a little more premium. Your mileage may vary though — if you can get your hands on a demo unit before buying one, if only to see the display, I’d recommend it. For everyone else, this price tag might have you waiting for the OLED panel rumored to be arriving on next year’s successor.

At $900 — or potentially more, considering this tablet actually launched at $850 — the Legion Tablet’s hardware leaves something to be desired. Biometrics are the most obvious weak spot; camera-based face unlock is the only biometric option provided here, without a fingerprint scanner in sight. I wouldn’t blame some if they saw this as a dealbreaker, because even if you’re willing to put up with the security concerns that a basic front-facing camera brings to the table, I can promise you you’ll be typing the code in all the time. Whether you’re trying to unlock in a darker environment or it’s just been four hours since you last unlocked the slate, make sure you have your pin memorized.

If you’re primarily buying this as a content consumption machine, I think you’ll mostly have a great time. Lenovo built stereo sound into this slate, but did so in one of the smartest ways I’ve ever seen, ensuring all speakers are located to the right side along the bottom and top of the device. This might sound a little unbalanced in portrait mode, but in landscape, it guarantees you’re never actually covering up the speakers while holding the slate in your hands, something practically every other equivalent tablet is guilty of. No headphone jack here, unfortunately, something I would’ve liked to have seen on a gaming-focused device.

I find battery life genuinely difficult to judge on tablets, especially if you don’t pick it up every single hour of every single day, but I’ve been impressed overall with the Legion Tab’s longevity. Some weekend YouTube binge watching sessions at relatively high brightness settings left me with plenty of juice in the tank, and its standby time between sessions has been excellent. Obviously, if you’re really pushing your graphical limits on this thing — or if you’re emulating non-native software through something like DroidDeck — you’re going to see a bit of a dip, but combined with fast charging and two USB-C ports for both portrait and landscape access, battery life gets a big thumbs-up from me.

Lenovo’s packed a couple of accessories in the box with this thing, but I wouldn’t get too excited. While it’s nice to see a 68W charger included for those aforementioned fast top-offs, the included folio case is just awful. Not only does the triangle-shaped flap barely stick together to form a proper stand, but one of the corners of my case was loose enough to frequently flex back and forth. Holding the tablet while wrapped in its case just felt bad, in the way that so many bundled cases often feel, and a quick look at Amazon shows that basically no recognizable brand — or unrecognizable, for that matter — has stepped up to offer something better.

The Legion Tablet also includes a screen protector in the box, if that’s how you roll.

Unsurprisingly, the Snapdragon 8 Elite Gen 5 in here can handle effectively anything you dare to throw at it. While my mobile games might be limited to more casual, indie fare like Balatro — and, by the by, if you’re still enjoying Balatro a couple of years later, the size and shape of this device makes it perfect — graphically-intense games played fine. Asphalt Legends or the HoYoverse games might not be my cup of tea, but they perform excellently on this device, and compared to the 12-inch tablets companies like Samsung and Amazon have become obsessed with, the display size here actually feels perfect.

We definitely have to talk about emulation, though, because it’s increasingly becoming the main focus of mobile gamers everywhere, and this feels like a tablet perfectly designed for something like DroidDeck. Getting Steam emulation — complete with the Deck’s usual Big Picture mode UI — available as an application on the Legion Tab Gen 5 takes all of two minutes and a handful of taps on your display, and I have to say, I’m pretty shocked at just how impressive the overall experience is right now.

DroidDeck gave me the option to bring my mini gaming tablet experience full-circle. While my Steam library has no shortage of games I think could run without stressing this slate, I wanted to push the envelope a little bit. Persona 3 Reload felt like as good a candidate as any: a modern title that doesn’t necessarily require quick response times and fits virtual controls quite nicely. Plus, most of my gaming time on Nvidia’s tablet a decade ago was spent working through Persona 3 Portable; like poetry, it rhymes. I installed the game, ran it on the Legion Tab, and to my surprise and delight, it was absolutely in a playable state.

Now, there are some caveats here. I did drop the game’s graphics preset to low, and even still, there were some occasional hiccups while running around an early dungeon. The game’s initial intros and disclaimers actually gave the impression the title wouldn’t run at all, but I was able to sneak up on a shadow, use a slash attack to begin the battle — basically the only thing in P3R that remotely requires accurate timing — and issue commands to my player character and party mates.

Obviously, your experience emulating Steam titles on the Legion Tab will vary on what you want to play, and with such wide variables between experiences, I probably wouldn’t push most gamers to run out and pick one up right away. It might be early days for DroidDeck, but considering how we’ve already seen it progress, I wouldn’t be surprised to see some buyers picking this up as a Valve-esque take on a gaming tablet.

With all of that power under the hood, though, it’s Lenovo’s take on Android that leaves a lot to be desired. It’s not just that this feels like a rough-and-ready take on AOSP, an unrefined package that would feel more in line with devices from a decade ago, though, yes, that’s half the struggle here. But at its worst, the Legion Tab Gen 5 simply feels unoptimized in daily use.

Scrolling through web pages or various apps often felt sluggish and heavy, like the UI elements on screen never actually match the movement of my finger, and it’s unclear how much of that was due to the refresh rate bug I described above. Auto-rotate was both super finnicky and super sluggish, alternating back and forth between one state or the other, and the auto-rotate button that pops up when it’s disabled would frequently fail to appear. Occasionally, I’d run into app issues, such as choppy playback on a 1440p YouTube video that got so bad — regardless of what resolution I selected — that I had to fully force close the app in settings to fix it. Even getting the slate to reliably wake up (or not wake up) when picking it up was annoying, as it’s simultaneously sensitive enough to frequently turn on when I don’t want it to, or to stay asleep when I’m trying to unlock it. Inevitably, I turned that setting off.

You might think that shelling out $900 for an Android tablet would buy you an out from pre-installed bloatware; you’d be wrong. During setup, the Legion Tab attempts to install a whole host of apps and games, including Perplexity, TikTok, Temu, several match-three games that I’ve truly never heard of, and no less than two mahjong and solitaire titles each. In fact, there might actually be three solitaire titles depending on what “Jigsawcard Solitaire Puzzle” is, but I unchecked all of these apps during setup and didn’t deem the title worthy of bothering to check.

While Lenovo has been trying to improve its software update policies on some recent devices, this slate isn’t among that group. To my knowledge, there is no official update schedule for this device, but based on past performance, I’d expect somewhere around 2 OS upgrades and 4 years of security patches, give or take.

I would’ve loved nothing more than to recommend this year’s Legion Tab to small tablet fans and gamers everywhere, but when this thing is $200 more than Apple’s latest iPad Mini — which in and of itself is absolutely overpriced — and $100 more than the base OLED Steam Deck post-price hike, I just have a hard time telling anyone to buy it. Really, its Steam performance was one of the few areas that made me feel truly excited by this thing, but it’s simply too unpredictable an experience at this point (and price) for me to recommend.

Even setting aside the price, there’s simply too much here that feels unfinished or rough around the edges for my liking. A handful of bug fixes and a couple of additional optimization passes could fix a lot of my more minor quibbles, but despite what I just said, you can only ignore the $900 price tag for so long before it bites you in the ass.

We have seen a post-iPad Mini discount bringing this slate to an Apple-matching $700 ahead of the holidays, but even that price is $150 more than its predecessor was at launch without a whole lot to show for it. If you’re looking for a gamer-friendly tiny tablet, I do think Legion is a brand worth keeping your eye on, especially as DroidDeck and other Steam emulation apps continue to evolve. But at this point — and for this price — your best bet is waiting for whatever model follows this one. It might still be stupid expensive, but at least you’ll be getting OLED.

A decade ago, Nvidia’s Shield Tablet felt like a no-brainer addition to my everyday carry. But in this economy, gamers and tablet fans alike are simply better off dropping $900 elsewhere.

#10396. The Design Workflow Is Broken. Here Is How Vitalii Khomenko Thinks It Should Be Fixed

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

With 250+ client engagements across both agency and embedded in-house roles, Vitalii Khomenko has seen product teams from the inside in a way few designers have. Here he makes the case for a new kind of design workflow built for the AI era, and explains why the window to adapt is narrower than most founders think.

For the last decade, every startup conversation about speed eventually became a conversation about engineering. Hiring more developers, cutting scope, choosing a simpler stack, it was almost always a dev-side problem. Vitalii Khomenko, Principal Designer at Orizon Design and founder of Fluente LLC, argues that's no longer true. And he has a specific view on what teams should do instead.

What makes Khomenko's perspective worth taking seriously isn't just seniority. It's the specific shape of his experience. Over a career spanning 250+ client engagements, he has worked both as an external specialist brought in to deliver and as an embedded designer who became the single connective thread between product, brand, and execution. That dual vantage point is rare. Most designers live on one side of that line. Khomenko has operated extensively on both, which gives him an unusually clear read on where friction actually originates inside product teams, and where it doesn't.

His work spans early-stage startups finding their first visual language and established brands managing complex, multi-disciplinary design systems. Across that range, certain patterns repeat. And the pattern that started becoming impossible to ignore this year pointed him toward a conclusion that is reshaping how he thinks a modern design workflow should actually be structured.

The bottleneck didn't disappear. It moved. And the workflow needs to move with it.

When shipping code was slow, the existing design workflow made sense. Multiple rounds of revision, user testing, iteration, all of that fit naturally into the window while engineering caught up. That window is now gone, and most design workflows were never updated to reflect it.

"Founders are asking design teams to move at the same speed as an AI-assisted dev pipeline, and design doesn't compress the same way code does," Khomenko says. The result is a workflow that was built around a constraint that no longer exists, applied to a pipeline that has fundamentally changed around it.

The forced choice between polish and speed that product teams are now navigating isn't new as a concept. What's new is how stark it has become, and how directly it traces back to a workflow problem rather than a people problem. "Because development is cheap and fast now, there's constant pressure to ship the next thing immediately," he explains. "But the products that actually retain users are still the ones where the details were considered carefully. Fewer revision rounds isn't a process improvement if it means shipping avoidable friction."

Khomenko has watched this play out across enough teams, at enough stages, to be specific about where the existing workflow breaks down. The issue isn't design judgment. It's where that judgment sits in the process, and what format it's expected to produce at the end.

To understand Khomenko's proposed fix, it helps to understand exactly where the current workflow stalls. For most product teams, the end of the design process looks like this: a designer produces polished, dev-ready screens inside Figma, and hands that file to engineering. Engineering then interprets those screens, translates the visual decisions into code, and builds from there.

That translation step, Figma file to working code, is where the pipeline now breaks down. AI-assisted engineering has made the building part dramatically faster. But the translation step hasn't changed. An engineer still has to read a Figma file, interpret spacing, extract assets, make judgment calls about component structure, and write the implementation from scratch. That process takes the same amount of time it always did.

"The manual workflow inside Figma simply isn't keeping up with AI-boosted engineering," Khomenko says. "The design handoff is quietly becoming the thing everyone is waiting on."

The result is a pipeline where everything around the handoff has accelerated, and the handoff itself has become the single point of drag. Figma, in other words, was built for a world where engineering was the slow part. It wasn't designed to feed a pipeline where engineering moves at AI speed.

This is the core of what Khomenko is proposing, and it's a more significant structural shift than it might first appear.

In his workflow, Figma doesn't disappear. It moves. Rather than serving as both the exploration environment and the final delivery artifact, Figma becomes the visual exploration layer exclusively: the place where brand identity is established, layout logic is worked through, and design direction is validated. It remains the right tool for figuring out what something should look and feel like.

But the final delivery, the artifact that actually goes to engineering, is code. Specifically, components built into prepared libraries that engineering can work from and scale directly, without any translation step in between.

The difference in implementation speed is the point. When a designer delivers a coded component library rather than a Figma file, engineering doesn't need to interpret anything. The decisions are already expressed in the language they're building in. What previously required an engineer to read, interpret, and re-create from a visual reference now arrives ready to use. In a pipeline running on AI-assisted development, that difference compounds fast.

"Figma is still the right place to explore what something should look and feel like," Khomenko says. "But handing over a Figma file and asking engineering to implement it is now the slow path. Delivering in code is the fast path. That's the shift."

Executing this workflow requires a different kind of designer, and Khomenko is direct about that. The UI/UX designer becoming a Design Engineer is, in his assessment, not a distant possibility. It is a matter of time, and the teams treating it as a future consideration rather than a present reality are the ones most likely to find themselves stuck at the handoff.

But Khomenko is equally clear about what this shift doesn't mean. Delivering in code is one part of the role, not the whole of it. The full design cycle isn't going anywhere. Applying branding to imagery, producing animations, building marketing materials, these remain essential creative tasks that require a broad and varied toolkit well beyond code. "The designer of the near future isn't just generating code," he says. "They're moving across the full cycle, brand, motion, marketing, product, with design engineering as an important and growing part of that stack, not a replacement for it."

What changes is that code-ready delivery becomes a core expectation within that stack. The designer who can move fluidly from visual exploration in Figma to component delivery in code, while also carrying the brand thinking, motion, and marketing creativity that differentiates products at the surface level users actually experience, is the profile that the new workflow demands.

Khomenko is candid that no single version of this workflow has fully standardized yet. Every team is figuring it out differently. The tools are evolving, the handoff expectations are shifting, and the processes teams are building around all of it vary considerably. There is no off-the-shelf playbook.

But this is precisely where his 250+ engagements become more than a credential. They're a dataset. Having watched enough teams, at enough stages, through enough cycles of tooling change, Khomenko can see through the noise of individual variation and identify what's directionally true even when the specifics are still in flux. "Each team's workflow is really custom at this stage," he says. "But some general patterns of how this is going to look in the near future are already visible."

Those patterns consistently point in the same direction: design judgment moving earlier, final delivery moving into code, and the designer's role expanding in technical range while staying broad in creative scope. The exact tools and sequences will keep shifting. The underlying logic of the workflow Khomenko is describing is already taking shape in the teams paying closest attention.

For founders and product leads, the workflow Khomenko is describing has direct implications for how teams are staffed, budgeted, and structured.

Front-load design judgment. The biggest leverage in the new workflow isn't faster execution, it's better problem definition before generation starts. Bring senior design thinking in earlier, when it can shape what gets built rather than polish what's already done.

Separate exploration from delivery. Figma and the visual exploration phase serve a distinct purpose from the final delivery layer. Treat them as separate stages with separate expectations, rather than collapsing them into a single step expected to produce everything.

Shift the delivery expectation to code. Whether that means hiring for Design Engineer range, developing it internally, or restructuring handoffs, the end of the design pipeline needs to arrive in a form engineering can work from directly. The translation step is where time goes to die, and it's now the most avoidable part of the process.

Protect the full creative stack. Design engineering matters more than it ever has. It is not, however, the whole job. Teams that reduce design to code generation will lose the brand thinking, motion, and marketing creativity that differentiates products at the surface level users actually experience.

Stay flexible. The workflow is still forming. Teams that build rigidity into their process now will find themselves redoing that work within a year. The teams getting this right are the ones building enough adaptability into their structures to absorb what's still coming.

"The Figma handoff made sense when engineering was the slow part," Khomenko says. "Engineering isn't the slow part anymore. Delivering in code isn't the future of design workflow. For the teams paying attention, it's already the present."

#10391. The iPhone Duo’s rocky launch is a reminder of how far Android foldables have come

Good morning. Today I’m finding open source alternatives to all of my apps, poring through Pitchfork’s list of the 100 best indie rock albums to find ones I haven’t listened to, and preparing my body for November 19th by pre-loading GTA VI (and replaying GTA IV in the meantime).

Inbox is written by Daniel Bader. Read previous issues here, and then catch up with Ben Schoon’s excellent Weekender column published every Sunday.

On Monday, I unboxed the iPhone Duo I pre-ordered back in October and experienced a disarming sense of déjà vu. It’s beautiful hardware married to a messy, often painful software onboarding experience atypical of Apple’s usual polish. It reminds me of the Galaxy Fold’s early years.

I was most looking forward to the combination of a foldable form factor and Apple’s superior app ecosystem, but even the apps that have been updated to support the new aspect ratio, on both the front and back, feel ungainly and buggy. In moving UI elements from the bottom to the right, even with the display open, the Duo forces a rebuilding of essential muscle memory that’s prevailed across mobile platforms, including Android foldables, for 20 years.

The front display, in particular, is a massive test of one’s patience: apps, even ones ostensibly optimized for its stout dimensions, often feel unusable, with UI elements that would be prominent on the iPhone 18 Pro hidden behind overflow menus on the Duo. It’s exasperating and, frankly, boggling.

But what’s interesting is the enthusiasm with which developers are approaching the new form factor. Reddit threads reveal dozens of Duo-optimized app recommendations that feel novel and, if not polished, then propulsive. There’s a fascinating delta between waiting for my bank, or Starbucks, or Kobo, to get its act together to support the Duo and seeking out net-new opportunities and experiences.

I spent most of the summer using the Galaxy Z Fold 8, and I didn’t expect going back to it to feel so stately and predictable: the showcase of a platform with fewer highs but none of the Duo’s current lows.

I’m not sure if I’m going to keep the Duo and wait out its colicky infancy. But I do know that until now I hadn’t given Samsung enough credit for the amount of work it did to make foldable software feel ordinary, and that’s the highest compliment I can pay.

This section has some of our favorite stuff from around the internet. Great videos, weird memes, fun reads, recommended apps, whatever. Send us your recommendations!

Andrew’s look at the Boox Picco is a nice reminder that a gadget can be interesting because it does less. The $109 e-reader has a 4-inch screen, physical page-turn buttons, and an included magnetic case that can attach to a compatible phone. It competes directly with XTEINK’s excellent X4 Pro, which features a rudimentary touchscreen and backlight for around $100, though its magnets are built in.

Both products are wonderful examples of hardware companies adapting to the growing size and complexity of smartphones and seeing an opening for single-purpose products that do one thing really well. Plus, it’s fun to stick a thing to the back of your phone and… read it.

For something you can explore without buying another device, Paolo Mainardi turned his personal website into a point-and-click adventure. Articles live in books, links sit on a notice board, and his about page is a letter in a lighthouse. The Context Island is an incredibly thoughtful and unique approach to “web” design, and it’s another example of how AI can really help bring crazy ideas to fruition.

We’ll likely find out today whether Jensen Huang will be removed as Nvidia’s CEO. According to the WSJ, the company’s board is preparing to oust the company’s singular chief executive, who co-founded it in 1993.

Momentum picked up after the New York Times reported that in addition to his three-year affair with Alice Huang, an alleged Chinese spy, Jensen had a relationship with one of his direct reports, who still works at the company.

A lot of people are rooting for Jensen Huang's return.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

A letter demanding the reinstatement of Jensen Huang as Nvidia CEO has been circulating online and has already garnered more than 91,000 signatures at the time of writing. At least one large online petition has circulated, with other big names supposedly among the signatories, ostensibly including Epic Games CEO Tim Sweeney and several prominent shareholders, partners, and current and former Nvidia employees. @yunta_tsai A petition is underway to reinstate Jensen and I’m signing it tooNovember 13, 2026

Jensen Huang’s fall was sudden and dramatic — he went from being one of the leading figures in the hardware needed to run AI to completely resigning from the company he built in under two weeks. It all started when U.S. intelligence sources claimed that Megaspeed executive Alice Huang, apparently close to the former CEO, was passing information to Chinese intelligence. The company initially denied this story, saying, “Megaspeed has been no different than many other regional cloud customers. Jensen’s interactions with Ms. Huang are not unusual or unexpected. Meetings with customers are part of a CEO's job; Jensen talks with thousands of customer executives every year.”

But a little over a week after that story, more news came out alleging that Jensen had an affair with the Megaspeed executive, forcing him to finally admit his relationship with the alleged spy. This led to a flurry of investigations by his own company’s board, the U.S. Congress, and the FBI. Just as things were already escalating, another alleged affair surfaced — this time with an Nvidia executive. Amid this whirlwind of scandals and rumors that Jensen would be ousted from the company, Elon Musk said it would be an honor to hire Jensen. Nvidia announced Jensen’s resignation just hours after Musk made the X post.

Despite the nature of his departure, it seems that there is still widespread support for Jensen from Nvidia’s employees, with thousands reportedly rallying behind him and calling for his reinstatement in an internal forum. The Stand with Jensen petition isn’t directly related to Nvidia, but it echoes comments from people inside the company that the former CEO was removed without meaningful due process. “Suspicion or association alone is not a sufficient basis for ending Jensen’s leadership,” the public letter said. “The inquiry must establish the facts, not merely justify an outcome already imposed.”

Jensen Huang’s larger-than-life persona will be difficult to replace, with the board considering candidates including Arm CEO Rene Haas and Apple Chief Hardware Officer Johny Srouji. It’s probably harder for interim CEO Colette Kress, who’s temporarily stepping into the position while the company is in the midst of massive turmoil, especially with the allegations that Huang was removed because of the mass smuggling of up to 1.5 million Nvidia AI GPUs, and who has the job of reporting its third-quarter results as CEO for the first time on November 17.

#10386. Google launches Intelligent Eyewear glasses: $395+, pre-order today, ships Dec. 10

After years of development, Google is launching its second foray into smart glasses with Intelligent Eyewear that supports Android and iOS. Pre-orders start today at Gentle Monster and Warby Parker, with the audio glasses shipping on December 10.

Hands-on: Google and Samsung’s vision for Android XR glasses is 20/20

Intelligent Eyewear is meant to be worn all day rather than the sunglasses-heavy approach taken by others. The frames are 40-44 grams with the weight balanced evenly.

There is a 12-megapixel ultra-wide camera for photos and videos (3K 30FPS or 1080p). You get image stabilization and HDR processing, while four microphones work to provide spatial audio capture. Google is also quite proud of its noise cancellation work.

There are two open-ear speakers for balanced audio and clear vocals. Control is done by a touchpad that accepts taps and swipes, or via voice commands, with Google debuting a new “Hey Gemini” hotword.

Another way to control Intelligent Eyewear is through your Pixel Watch or Galaxy Watch, with Fitbit Air support coming next year:

Notably, Gemini commands that invoke the camera — like visual search or translation — will not save images or videos to your Gemini History. These visuals won’t be used to train AI models or for ad targeting. Additionally, the glasses will “automatically redact bystanders’ faces from these visuals before Gemini processes them.”

Battery life (245mAh) is rated at nine hours on a single charge, with a fully topped-up charging case (Gentle Monster’s shown below) offering seven full charges. Power is transferred via contact pins located on the underside of the nose bridge.

Android XR runs on the Qualcomm Snapdragon AR1 Gen 1 with 2GB of RAM and 64GB of storage. Other specifications include Bluetooth 5.4, Wi-Fi 6E, and IPX4 water resistance. Samsung is responsible for engineering the hardware, while there are two eyewear brands at launch.

Warby Parker Intelligent Eyewear is available in three styles and a total of 14 variations. Alden, Reid, and Wren are inspired by the brand’s “most beloved silhouettes.” Pricing starts at $395 for optical and sun styles in Pine, Saddle, and Jet Black.

The Gentle Monster Intelligent Eyewear collection features six frames in bold colors like dark red, pink, gray, and yellow, with 16 total variations. They can be paired with tinted, clear, blue, or brown lenses, with prescription options available for most designs. Pricing starts at $449 and will be available in the US and South Korea.

Intelligent Eyewear can be used for contextual visual searches, asking Gemini about your day, and having it accomplish tasks.

Google Maps offers turn-by-turn directions, while there’s real-time Google Translate in over 70 languages.

Like on phones, Gemini Guided Vision “offers conversational audio descriptions to people with vision impairments.” This can be used to read fine print, identify color differences, find items, and explore unfamiliar places.

#10385. 7 Best Accounts Receivable (AR) Software Platforms for Automating Invoice-to-Cash

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

The best accounts receivable software for your company is the layer that closes the stage where your cash leaks. If buyers settle from an emailed link and outreach starts late, a reminders tool covers most of it. If customers prefer to pay online by card or ACH, add a payments network with a customer portal. If clients require invoice uploads to Coupa or Ariba and clear multiple invoices with one blank-memo wire, you need an AR automation layer on top of the ERP. Every platform listed here serves B2B businesses invoicing other companies, excluding consumer payment apps and collection agencies.

Our source material is each vendor's public product, integration, security and pricing pages, plus G2 reviews, as they stood in November 2026.

About three in five US suppliers let business clients pay within 30 days, per Atradius's 2026 US payment practices survey (September 2026). Most overdue invoices settle within a month past due, so funds arrive roughly two months post-billing. The same survey notes US enterprises often lose 1% to 2% of B2B invoices to bad debt. Software that shortens this window performs four main functions:

What invoice-to-cash software does not handle is core billing operations. Billing schedules, revenue recognition, and GL accounting remain within the ERP. Contract-first platforms initiate workflows from signed agreements and map customer billing prior to collection efforts; invoice-first applications pick up after ERP invoice generation.

Most finance stacks have four layers: the ERP that creates the invoice and holds the books, a payments network, a reminders tool, and an AR automation layer working the steps in between.

The ERP covers the last row, and a reminders tool covers timing. The rows in between depend on reading what a customer or a portal sent, which is AR automation work. That share keeps growing. Paper checks fell to 26% of B2B payments in 2025, per the AFP 2025 Digital Payments Survey as reported by Nacha, and FedNow settled about 2.5 times as many payments in Q3 2026 as a year earlier, per Federal Reserve FedNow statistics. Heading into 2027, more money arrives over electronic rails while its remittance detail lands somewhere else.

Layer: AI-native AR automation, for Series A to Fortune 500 finance teams

Published pricing: Pricing scales with business volume and isn't a percentage of collections

Leak it closes: Unapplied cash first, then stalled replies and portal rejections.

Layer: AR automation with built-in payments, sold as Invoiced by Flywire

Leak it closes: Cash application, where CashMatch AI handles wires that pay several invoices at once and payments that arrive without remittance.

Leak it closes: Invoice delivery into buyer portals, through 260+ AP portal integrations run from one interface.

Layer: AR automation layer with AI agents, on the ERP

Leak it closes: Customer replies that carry a promise-to-pay date or a dispute.

Layer: Payments network with customer portal

Leak it closes: Payment acceptance, through a customer portal for paying and disputing invoices online.

Layer: Reminders tool with AI reply drafts, on Xero and QuickBooks

Published pricing: Essentials $69 a month; Pro $99 to $799 a month; Custom from $999 a month

Leak it closes: Replies that sit unanswered at small and midsize companies.

Published pricing: US $259, $779 or $1,169 a month by annual revenue band; annual billing saves 10%

Leak it closes: Follow-up timing, with reminders sent from your own email address on a schedule that escalates.

On Xero or QuickBooks with customers paying from the emailed link, the leak tends to be timing, and a reminders tool covers it. Once W-9 requests and payment questions fill your team's week, the replies need a different layer.

Portal rejections and lump-sum wires put the leak in the table's middle rows, where an AR automation layer on the ERP fits. In a demo, we'd ask each vendor to show how fast its software flagged a rejected portal invoice, then to apply a multi-invoice wire with a blank memo live.

If your team still keys card numbers or chases ACH details, start with the payments layer. A payments network with a customer portal gives buyers stored methods and autopay. Unless the vendor says otherwise, it won't read replies or file into buyer portals, so plan an AR layer for those stages.

Whichever of the best accounts receivable software platforms you pick, it sits on top of the ERP. Four steps keep the books intact:

#10384. Dev hacks Coway air purifier to cut it off from the cloud

Claude refused to help, ChatGPT obliged, and Coway’s app is no longer in control.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

A developer discovered a clever way to cut his Coway Airmega IconS air purifier off from the cloud with the help of a root certificate, allowing local control. The method took advantage of a security hole — the fan downloads the certificate over plain HTTP — and now his Home Assistant server stands in for Coway’s cloud. The result: no more dealing with the unreliable integration that ran through Coway’s IoCare cloud, which broke “at least once or twice a year.”

The developer, Scott J. Goldman, detailed why and how he did it, starting with replacing a dumb Airmega that was “an overpriced box fan with a HEPA filter and an air quality sensor.” The replacement Airmega IconS, which is now discontinued but has Wi-Fi support, had inconsistent Home Assistant (HA) integration. He decided to take matters into his own hands with AI, although he noted that Claude’s guardrails blocked his request to set up his Raspberry Pi to intercept the fan’s traffic under the cybersecurity category, “not even for… uhh… testing purposes.” ChatGPT obliged and wrote the firewall rules, and an AI he doesn’t name later rebuilt the fan’s Bluetooth setup in about 20 minutes.

The fan’s inconsistency had a reason: the IconS has no official HA support, so the route around that relied on a community integration that logs into Coway’s IoCare+ cloud. Coway originally banned the integration for overloading its servers before allowing it after a community dispute. Coway also rebranded its app and cloud from IoCare to IoCare+, which forced a rewrite. Even small HTML layout changes on the pages the integration scraped could break it. On top of that, the company’s servers went offline here and there.

Worse, the developer’s account was banned for running an old integration version, and he lacked remote control for weeks. Originally, all he wanted was for Apple Home’s Good Night scene to switch the purifier on. A common complaint about Internet of Things (IoT) devices like this is that you’re at the mercy of the manufacturer, and support can later evaporate. AI has helped some users like this take back control.

His method involved using the purifier’s security lapse against itself. At setup, it fetches the certificate it trusts over plain, unencrypted HTTP, which can be intercepted and replaced using mitmproxy/mitmweb with nftables port redirection. The request he saw was a plain HTTP GET for AmazonRootCA1.pem from an S3 bucket in AWS’s Seoul region. He then had his proxy serve its own CA to the fan, and the fan made HTTPS requests to Coway’s cert server, which included downloads of a device certificate and private key. Another proxy plugin then swapped his own key into that response.

The fan’s commands ran over MQTT, a lightweight messaging protocol common in smart-home devices, secured with TLS on port 8883 and routed through AWS IoT. The proxy doesn’t decode MQTT, but it showed the raw stream, which the unnamed AI mapped to his app button presses.

He had first considered exploiting eMudhra, a certificate authority whose private keys turned out to be trivially weak last month, which would have involved forging a certificate. That wasn’t necessary because of the plain-HTTP download. Self-signed certificates are nothing new to enthusiasts; this just took a little extra work and avoided the eMudhra plan’s reliance on several things lining up.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

Doing the Bluetooth work by hand would have taken hours or even days, he estimated. The unnamed AI also drove an Android phone over adb to read its Bluetooth logs and reconstruct the protocol.

The project is offered as coway-local, which allows the fan to be connected to HA directly, and from there to Apple Home. A Web Bluetooth page, openable in Chrome on a Mac or Android device near the fan, sends the Wi-Fi details. That Bluetooth setup also carries the fan’s certificate URLs, so coway-local points them at the HA server and no traffic interception is needed. The Wi-Fi password goes to the fan over Bluetooth; HA never stores it, and the setup link expires after 15 minutes. The integration runs its own MQTT broker, so the fan now talks to HA instead of AWS.

HA Core 2026.10.2 or newer is needed with a fixed LAN address and 2.4 GHz Wi-Fi. There’s a catch: using it disconnects the fan from Coway’s cloud, so Coway’s app is no longer able to control it. It’s also experimental and tested only on the IconS (AP-1022K), and other Coway models may get through Bluetooth setup without actually working.

The features list is incomplete: PM10 and other air-quality metrics, filter alerts, and firmware updates aren’t implemented yet. He suspects Coway’s cloud could eventually be discontinued or come with a service fee, and a local path is insurance against that. At least for him, this brings satisfaction, as “Now, I can finally click Good Night.”

#10380. Hands-on: Google and Samsung’s vision for Android XR glasses is 20/20

While I acknowledge Meta’s role in bringing smart glasses to a mainstream audience, it has proven to be an irresponsible steward. Its “growth at all costs” mindset has actively poisoned people’s perception of the form factor. Under Meta’s watch, glasses have gained a creepy connotation before their utility has been fully realized.

Google’s second take on smart glasses is the exact opposite of what Meta has done. Every single aspect has been considered, and the result is worthy of being most people’s introduction to the form factor.

Officially, Android XR glasses are known as Gentle Monster and Warby Parker Intelligent Eyewear. The branding is a mouthful, and I’m not quite sure “Intelligent Eyewear” will take off. Something more colloquial, like “Google glasses” — glasses.google is the landing page — or “Samsung glasses,” will probably stick in the public consciousness.

That critique aside, the name reflects how Google wants Intelligent Eyewear to actually replace your existing glasses. Unlike the Ray-Ban trap that I frankly think Meta did not consider, Google’s offerings are not situational or activity-based devices like sunglasses. These are a “smart” upgrade to the assistive device some people have to wear.

As a wearable, smart glasses are a bit like smartwatches in that the platonic design is pretty well established and indistinguishable from traditional eyewear. Unlike watches, glasses are so visible on people’s faces that form overrides everything else.

Fortunately, the Samsung hardware I wore for an hour last week looks and feels like glasses, almost unremarkably so. I’d venture to say that audio-only glasses without a display are a solved design challenge. Google emphasized that, at 41 grams, this is the lightest pair of smart glasses on the market, and it worked to make sure the weight is distributed evenly.

The sides of the stems that face your head feature translucent plastic that lets you see the internal components. I liked that small design choice. You can control the glasses using voice commands, with Google debuting a new “Hey Gemini” hotword for the form factor. While “Hey Google” and “OK Google” remain available, the new one is meant to reflect how big a role Gemini plays on smart glasses.

There is also a touchpad on the right stem that accepts taps and swipes, while a button up top lets you take pictures and record video. Another way you’ll be able to control the glasses is with your Pixel Watch and Galaxy Watch. You can play/pause audio and enable Gemini using double-pinch and wrist gestures. Notably, gesture support is also coming to the Fitbit Air next year.

From its internal testing, Google tells me that which input method people use is pretty situational and ends up being natural. At the beginning of my demo, I favored the touchpad, but by the end, I was leaning toward voice commands.

Talking to Intelligent Eyewear uses Gemini Live. Some prompts, like take a picture or play a song, are one-shot “chat” commands in phone parlance, but for the most part, you’re using Gemini’s back-and-forth conversation mode. Google heavily customized the Gemini Live model for glasses to reduce latency, shorten responses, and tightly integrate the camera.

Even compared to my last demo in May, the latency of voice commands has come a long way. That said, I need more time to see if it’s really fast enough to use as a primary interaction method for most commands.

Similarly, Google will blur all faces and filter out bystander voices before it gets uploaded to Gemini in the cloud. This on-device processing occurs on your phone.

Since that visual media isn’t being saved, the external indicator light won’t be active when you’re talking to Gemini. The LED is only on when photos or videos are being saved and can be reviewed later.

That indicator light features anti-tamper detection that prevents recording. Algorithms detect whether the LED is covered by stickers or markers, with this process featuring hardware verification when the glasses are in their charging case.

Google’s “Glasses” companion app, with Samsung having a near-identical version for Galaxy phones, is pretty similar to the version for Pixel Watch. Unlike the bloated “Meta AI” app, it’s just for settings rather than the very definition of a growth hack with a full chatbot. The difference is staggering, and something Google deserves a bit of credit for in this age of throwing AI into every crevice of day-to-day smartphone usage.

Under the hood, the glasses are offloading a lot of processing to your phone. This includes HDR pipelines and video stabilization. The 12MP camera takes pictures in a 4:3 aspect ratio, while you can record up to 90 seconds of video. Images are transferred immediately to your phone over Wi-Fi, while videos wait until the glasses are charging to preserve battery life. I am so excited by point-of-view photography and the instantaneous capture the glasses offer, and early impressions of camera quality are positive.

Another thing I want to test is how Intelligent Eyewear fares as always-available headphones. There are two open-ear speakers and four microphones, and Google is clearly very proud of the noise-cancellation work in place.

As someone who has been waiting for smart glasses since 2012, I cannot overstate my annoyance at how Meta allowed smart glasses to gain a scummy undertone, if not overtone. It should have been more proactive to societal issues. Meanwhile, Meta releasing glasses without a camera, thus removing the primary utility of real-world context, makes me deeply question its belief that this is the future.

Smart glasses that know what you’re interacting with in the world can be extremely useful. That always-available camera is a genuine physical advantage over smartphones. It unlocks new use cases and convenience that are just not possible with something that goes in your pocket.

Google took a long time to get back to glasses. From what I’ve used so far, the wait will be worth it. The hardware and designs are solid, while the software is chock-full of innovative, well-considered ideas.

#10379. Here’s how Google’s Android XR glasses are handling the camera & privacy

It’s a shame, because a camera in smart glasses serves such a functional role, both for the user in just capturing pictures and for the AI assistant built into the experience. I recently talked about how the camera was my favorite part of smart glasses in an issue of our 9to5Google Weekender newsletter, but how it just feels a bit wrong now.

Anyway, with that context in mind, let’s look at how Google is handling this touchy subject.

Importantly, the light on each pair of Android XR glasses is also designed to avoid any tampering. Android XR glasses automatically perform a system check to ensure the light is working on a regular basis – you might catch the light blinking in white and blue while charging which is a part of the system check ensuring the hardware is fully functional. If the light is found not to be working, the camera is disabled.

Continuing on with how the camera interacts with Gemini, Google explained in a conversation with 9to5Google that Gemini is tuned not to use the camera in cases where it’s not obvious that the user wants the camera to be active. An interaction such as “what painting is this” would very clearly imply that the user wants the glasses to view the scene in front of them and identify the art piece, where “how much money is on the table,” Google explained, would ask for a follow-up before using the camera seeing as it’s not obvious that the user expects the camera to be used in that sort of phrase.

Another way in which Android XR glasses will avoid the recording stigma is with their limits. Google is limiting Intelligent Eyewear video recording to 90-second sessions. While that does limit what the user can do, Google said that most recordings fell within that window anyway. Meta, by comparison, offers recordings up to 5 minutes.

Microbiologists modified an Ender 3 FDM printer to print macrostructures from microbes and make research on engineered living materials more accessible.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Microbiologists at the University of Rochester modified an Ender 3 FDM 3D printer into a 3D bioprinter for engineered living material (ELM) research. The team used SolidWorks to create the 3D model and sliced it in Slic3r, akin to a normal 3D printing workflow, with modified settings for the custom syringe pump print head. The $450 3D bioprinter makes ELM research more accessible, as bioprinters usually cost over $100,000.

Synthetic biology is like combining engineering with microbiology, treating microbes as machines and physical building blocks to achieve various functions. In the journal ACS Applied Polymer Materials, biologist Anne S. Meyer et al. published a 3D Bioprinting of Engineered Living Materials paper in August. The paper says that synthetic biology and 3D bioprinting have advanced ELM research, but the hardware costs and the inability of the expensive hardware to maintain the structure limit further progress. Hitting two birds with one stone, the researchers built an open-source, affordable 3D bioprinter from an Ender 3 and created their own support slurry, allowing the ELMs to keep their shape.

The researchers kept most of the 3D printer's structure, mainly replacing the print head with a syringe pump mounted with custom 3D-printed PETG components, designed to utilize a 10 mL glass Luer-lock syringe with a plunger mechanism to control extrusion and retraction. The workflow is very similar to normal 3D printing, with the shape created with CAD using SolidWorks, exported as an STL, and sliced in Slic3r. The researchers tweaked the print settings in Slic3r to match the 0.254mm needle, with the extrusion multiplier dialed down all the way to 0.004, resulting in a flow rate of 2 mL/h at a print speed of 23 mm/s.

Instead of a filament, the researchers created a bio-ink from a mix of bacteria and alginate to be printed inside two types of support material: Agarose or gelatin gels. Agarose is used for long-term incubation, with the printed ELM staying viable for four or more weeks, and gelatin is used for extractable prints. The support slurries are enriched with Calcium ions, which cause the alginate strands to cross-link, creating a solid structure that encapsulates the living bacteria.

ELMs are not limited to bacteria, but the paper mainly experiments with bacteria to explore bacteria-generated biomaterials with desirable characteristics such as biofilms for antimicrobial treatments, plastic degradation, or even wastewater treatment — or bacterial cellulose with structural characteristics to be used in applications such as a biodegradable plastic alternative. With the team’s efforts to make ELM research more affordable, developments could happen faster, with many applications across many fields, possibly even bacteria-based bioplastic 3D printing filaments for FDM printers.

#10374. Pixel Watch and Fitbit Air will be able to control Android XR glasses with gestures

Shipments of data center CPUs set records.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Shipments of x86 CPUs declined quarter-over-quarter and year-over-year in the third quarter as demand for client processors dropped sharply due to high component prices, while server CPU unit sales grew by a whopping 20% year-over-year, according to the latest report from Mercury Research. Intel regained overall CPU market share from AMD for the first time since Q2 2025, largely due to surprisingly strong performance in desktop PCs. Meanwhile, AMD continued to set new records in the mobile and server CPU segments, the report claims. In total, the industry supplied 68.93 million x86 CPUs, Jon Peddie Research reports.

The overall CPU market contracted both sequentially and year-over-year in unit shipments in Q3 2026, after a much stronger-than-expected second quarter, according to Mercury Research. AMD and Intel shipped 60.8 million client CPUs and 8.13 million data center processors during the quarter. One thing to keep in mind about the reports from JPR and Mercury Research is that they count CPU shipments, which happen months before those CPUs are sold in actual systems.

"After a far better than expected second quarter market for client processors, the third quarter PC CPU market declined far more than expected, entirely due to a downturn in mobile CPUs — this was the same segment that was unexpectedly higher last quarter, hinting that part of the downturn may have been due to CPU demand pulling in sales from the third quarter," said Dean McCarron, principal analyst at Mercury Research.

"Both Intel and AMD indicated in their earnings results that there was a rapid softening in PC demand late in the third quarter that impacted client processor shipments and that the outlook for client CPUs in the fourth quarter will be a decline in excess of 20%. Unlike client CPUs, however, server processors continued to grow strongly in the past quarter, as did embedded CPUs."

Quarter over quarter, the decline was driven primarily by significantly lower demand for mobile processors, as 86 notebook CPU shipments dropped by more than 10%. By contrast, desktop CPU shipments recovered, largely thanks to Intel, while server and embedded CPU shipments continued to grow strongly. Excluding IoT, semi-custom SoCs, and embedded products, both AMD and Intel shipped fewer x86 processors than in the previous quarter, though AMD's decline was larger than Intel's.

Compared with the third quarter of 2025, CPU shipments declined across all major market segments except servers. Desktop and notebook CPU shipments decreased due to weaker PC demand amid rising component costs, particularly memory prices. Embedded processor and semi-custom SoC shipments also declined, primarily due to AMD's shrinking game console business. By contrast, fueled by the ongoing buildout of AI infrastructure, server CPU shipments increased by more than 20% YoY, their highest annual growth rate in nearly five years.

"Client CPU shipments softened in Q3 as OEMs kept inventories lean and the DIY channel remained weak," said Dr. Jon Peddie, president of Jon Peddie Research. "Server CPUs continued growing, with AMD gaining share and AI-related demand supporting the market. We expect PC conditions to remain difficult into the first half of 2027."

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

Due to weakness in the client PC space, AMD's overall x86 market share (excluding embedded and semi-custom SoC) declined by 0.6% QoQ for the first time in years, but still grew by a significant 4.5% YoY, according to Mercury Research.

In the x86 client PC segment, Intel regained some ground on AMD in Q3 2026 as overall client CPU shipments declined more sharply than expected, according to both Jon Peddie Research and Mercury Research. JPR estimates that AMD and Intel shipped 60.8 million client processors in Q3 2026, down from 62.93 million a quarter before and roughly 65 million in Q3 2025.

AMD's client CPU unit share dropped to 29.3%, down from a record 30.3% in Q2, but still notably higher than the 25.4% it commanded a year earlier. Meanwhile, Intel increased its share to 70.7%, though its position remained considerably weaker than in Q3 2025, when it controlled 74.6% of the market.

Intel's sequential market share gains were driven entirely by a substantial increase in desktop CPU shipments, while AMD continued to gain ground in notebooks, albeit at a much slower pace than in previous quarters. Mercury Research believes that weaker demand for mobile CPUs may have enabled Intel to allocate more silicon to desktop processors, a market that the company had undersupplied for some time.

However, the outlook for both companies' client CPU businesses is rather gloomy, as rising component costs (especially DRAM and 3D NAND prices) are weighing on PC demand. Both AMD and Intel anticipate a sequential decline in client CPU shipments of more than 20% in the fourth quarter and further declines in the year to come. Meanwhile, one has to keep in mind that CPUs sold in Q4 are meant for systems to be shipped in Q1, which is traditionally weak. Hence, while the 20% YoY decline is extraordinary, a substantial part of that decline is seasonal.

In the desktop PC segment, Intel made a spectacular comeback in Q3 2026, reclaiming 5% of the market in one quarter. Intel's desktop CPU unit share climbed to 70.1%, up 5% sequentially and 3.7% in the same quarter a year ago. By contrast, AMD's share dropped to 29.9%, down from 34.9% in Q2 2026 and 33.6% in Q3 2025, meaning the company wiped out its market share gains over the past year.

Mercury Research speculates that weaker demand for notebook processors enabled Intel to allocate more silicon to mainstream desktop CPUs, a market that both companies have underserved for some time. Another factor behind AMD's poor performance was its greater exposure to the gaming PC market, which suffered from limited availability of gaming graphics cards and unusually high costs for other components, such as memory modules and SSDs.

Yet, desktops became the only major x86 CPU market segment where AMD lost unit share both sequentially and year-over-year.

In the mobile PC segment, both AMD and Intel saw a substantial decline in CPU shipments in Q3 2026 as the market contracted more than 10% sequentially, according to Mercury Research. However, AMD's shipments declined slightly less than Intel's, which enabled the company to increase its mobile CPU unit share to a record 29.2%, up from 28.9% in Q2 2026 and 21.9% in Q3 2025. Intel remained the dominant supplier with a 70.8% share, down from 71.1% in the previous quarter and 78.1% a year earlier.

Although AMD's sequential market share gain of 0.2% was negligible, its 7.2% YoY gain was impressive, especially given Intel's historically strong position in the notebook market. Mercury Research attributes the sharp sequential decline in mobile CPU shipments partly to PC makers pulling forward their CPU procurements into the unusually strong second quarter.

Unfortunately for both AMD and Intel, the outlook for the fourth quarter is gloomy, as rising memory and other component prices drive up notebook prices and reduce end-user demand.

Unlike the bleak client CPU market, server CPUs are thriving, with shipments of data center processors reaching 8.13 million units in Q3 2026, according to Jon Peddie Research. The market again grew 20% year-over-year, the highest annual growth rate in nearly five years, according to Mercury Research.

In the server CPU segment, AMD continued its winning spree as its unit share reached a record 35.5%, up from 34.5% in Q2 2026 and 27.8% in Q3 2025. Intel remained the dominant supplier with a 64.5% share, down 1% sequentially and 7.7% year-over-year.

Interestingly, Intel managed to slow AMD's advance in the core data center CPU market by apparently substantially increasing shipments of its Xeon SP processors, McCarron believes. When the comparison is limited to AMD's EPYC and Intel's Xeon SP processors, AMD's unit share reached 46.8%, up just 0.4% sequentially, which represents AMD's smallest sequential gain over the past year, as the company's quarterly share gains by this metric averaged 3% over the preceding three quarters.

Unlike the client CPU market, the outlook for the server processor market remains particularly favorable for both AMD and Intel. The ongoing buildout of AI infrastructure drives demand not only for conventional data center-grade processors, but also for workstation-class CPUs used in smaller departmental AI servers.

Shipments of x86 CPUs declined both sequentially and year-over-year in Q3 2026 to 68.93 million units as rising component prices weighed on client PC demand, while server CPU shipments grew more than 20% year-over-year.

Intel regained overall x86 CPU market share from AMD for the first time since Q2 2025, largely thanks to a spectacular comeback in the desktop CPU segment, where its share reached 70.1%. Meanwhile, AMD gained 0.3% of the mobile CPU market, now commanding 29.2%, its highest share ever. Furthermore, AMD reached a record unit share in the server CPU segment, where it now commands 35.5%.

Both companies expect client CPU shipments to drop by more than 20% sequentially in Q4, whereas demand for server processors continues to grow due to the ongoing buildout of AI infrastructure.

#10369. What is Model Routing in AI?

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

"Do you know the closest ATM?" and "I was charged twice, the refund never arrived, and now my card is blocked" are both customer messages. Most AI apps send both to the same large model and pay large-model prices for each one.

Model routing fixes that. A router looks at each request and sends it to the cheapest model that can handle it, and keeps the expensive model for the requests that need it.

In this guide we'll cover what model routing is, how routers decide, and what it saves. Then we'll build a small router in about 60 lines of JavaScript and run it over 200 customer messages. At one setting it matched a large model's accuracy for about a third of the cost. At another it came within a point and a half for 89% less.

Model routing in AI is the practice of sending each request to the model best suited to it, instead of sending every request to one model. A router sits between your app and a pool of models, reads each request, and picks a model based on what you care about: cost, speed, or quality.

You'll also see it called AI model routing, LLM routing, or LLM model routing. They all mean the same thing. The pool might be a small and a large model from one provider, or models from several providers behind one API.

The idea rests on one observation: most requests are easy. A small model answers them as well as a large one, in a fraction of the time and for a fraction of the price. The router's whole job is finding the hard ones.

The diagram below shows a typical AI model routing architecture.

Model routing works in three steps: the router reads the request, decides which model should answer it, and forwards the request to that model. The middle step is where routers differ. Routing tools decide which model to use in one of four ways.

Rule-based routing uses rules you write by hand. Requests from free-plan users go to a small model. Anything tagged as code goes to a coding model. Prompts over a certain length go to a model with a bigger context window. It's simple, predictable, and free to run, but the rules only know what you thought of in advance.

Intelligent model routing uses a model to make the decision. A small, fast classifier reads each request and predicts which model in the pool will answer it well. You'll also see it called smart or dynamic model routing.

Two more terms come up here. Task-aware routing picks a model by the kind of job, like summarising, coding, or answering a support question. Context-aware routing also looks at what surrounds the request, like the conversation so far, the user, or the tools available.

The big platforms work this way. Microsoft describes its Foundry model router as "a trained language model" that picks a model for each prompt in real time. Google says Gemini Notebook uses its new Decisions API as a real-time router, serving everyday queries with up to 40% lower latency. I've tested the Google Decisions API against Jev on the same messages we'll use below.

Cascade routing tries the cheapest model first and only moves up when the answer isn't good enough. The usual test is confidence: if the cheap model is sure, keep its answer. If it isn't, send the request to a stronger model. It's also called confidence-based routing, and it's what we'll build below.

A cascade needs no separate classifier, because the first model's own confidence does the routing. The trade-off is that escalated requests pay for two models and wait for both.

Fallback routing is about staying up, not saving money. If a model times out, hits a rate limit, or its provider has an outage, the router sends the request to the next model on a list. Most production routers combine fallbacks with one of the three methods above.

The main benefits of model routing in AI systems are lower cost, faster responses, and fewer outages, without giving up quality on the hard requests.

We'll build a cascade router for a job every support tool has: sorting customer messages. Each message gets one of ten labels, like atm support or request refund.

The test set is 200 messages from Banking77, a public set of banking support messages: ten labels, 20 messages each. They're the same 200 messages from my zero shot classification test, which is where the single-model numbers below come from.

Put both keys in a .env file and run the examples with node --env-file=.env. It's the same setup as in my guide to getting a YouTube API key.

Create router.mjs. The first function sends the message to Jev with one multiple-choice question, and returns the label Jev picked along with its confidence:

Confidence runs from 0 to 1. When nearly all the probability sits on one label, it's close to 1. When Jev is torn between two labels, it drops.

The second function asks Claude Opus 5.5 the same question. The output_config block limits the answer to one of our labels, so there's no free text to parse. SitePoint's guide to LLM structured output with JSON Schema explains how that works.

The router itself is about a dozen lines:

If Jev is at least 80% confident, its answer goes straight back. If not, Claude answers instead. And if Claude fails, the router falls back to Jev's answer, because a shaky answer beats an error page.

Try it on an easy message and a harder one:

Jev was sure about the lost card. On the ATM question it leaned towards card acceptance with a confidence of 0.64, so the router asked Claude, which got it right.

Because cheap, strong, and threshold are options, the same function routes between any two models.

Model routing reduces AI costs by keeping easy requests on a cheap model and paying for the expensive one only when it's needed. Here's what that looked like on our 200 messages, starting with each model on its own:

Opus gets six more messages right out of 200, and costs more than 120 times as much.

Now the router, at four confidence thresholds:

Speed improved too. At the 0.8 threshold, the messages Jev kept came back in a median of 227 ms. The ones that escalated took about 2.1 seconds, because they waited for both models.

A note on method: I ran every message through both models once and saved the answers, then replayed the router over them at each threshold. That's how you compare thresholds without paying for the same test four times.

Then I ran the router live to check. At 0.95 it scored the same 95.0% and sent 61 messages to Opus. At 0.8 it scored 93.5% and sent 20, a point under the replay. Two answers moved: one of Jev's confidence scores landed at 0.82 instead of 0.78, just over the line, and Opus changed its answer on one message. Scores near the threshold shift a little between runs, so leave yourself a margin.

The choice of cheap model matters less than you'd think. With Google's Gemini Decision 1 in first place instead of Jev, a 0.7 threshold reached the same 95% for US$0.28 per 1,000 messages.

Model routing can cost a little quality, and the threshold decides how much. In our test the gap to Opus ranged from nothing at 0.95 to two points at 0.7.

The loss comes from one place: answers the cheap model got wrong while feeling sure. At the 0.8 threshold, Jev kept five wrong answers because its confidence was above the line. Opus got four of those five wrong as well, so only one was a real loss: "Do all ATMs take this card?", which Jev filed under card acceptance with a confidence of 0.91.

Escalating isn't risk-free either. On one message, Jev had the right label with a confidence of 0.44, and Opus overruled it with the wrong one.

To optimize costs using AI model routing, measure first, route second, and keep measuring. These six steps cover LLM model routing cost optimization for any pair of models:

The return is easy to estimate once you know the split. If nine in ten requests stay on a model that costs 1% as much, the bill drops by about 89%, which is what we measured.

Several inference providers have multi-model routing built in, and most AI gateway platforms support model routing too, so you don't have to write your own. As of November 2026, these are the top model routing solutions for AI apps. Each name links to the tool's model routing docs:

Hosted model routing platforms for large language models decide for you, which is the fastest way to start. A hand-built router like ours takes more work, but you choose the signal and you can see why every request went where it did.

An LLM router is the component that does model routing for large language models. It can be a function in your own code, like the one in this guide, a hosted model such as Microsoft Foundry's model router, or a feature of an AI gateway.

Smart model routing is another name for intelligent model routing: a model, not a hand-written rule, decides where each request goes. Dynamic model routing means the same thing.

The ROI of AI model routing depends on how much of your traffic a cheaper model can handle. In our test, a 0.8 threshold kept 89.5% of messages on the cheap model and cut the cost per 1,000 messages from US$1.96 to US$0.22, for about a point of accuracy. The build was about 60 lines of code.

OpenRouter's auto model routing works through its Auto Router. You set the model to openrouter/auto, and a decision model picks a model and a reasoning effort for each request. You pay the standard rate of whichever model it selects, with no extra fee.

OpenClaw model routing is a primary model plus an ordered list of fallbacks. In the OpenClaw model routing configuration you set agents.defaults.model.primary and agents.defaults.model.fallbacks, and an agent can override the fallback list. When a model fails, OpenClaw first rotates auth profiles on the same provider, then moves to the next model on the list, as its model failover docs describe. That makes it fallback routing, not cost-based routing.

An AI gateway simplifies model routing by putting every model behind one API, one set of keys, and one place for logs. Your app calls the gateway, and the routing rules live in config instead of code, so changing a model doesn't need a deploy.

Mid-stream failover is when a router switches models after a streamed response has already started, because the first model failed partway through. It's the hardest kind of failover, because the user has already seen part of an answer. Many routers only fail over before the first token arrives and return an error after that, so check what your gateway does.

#10367. Two HPE Juniper network routers grounded all US flights on Friday
