New gear sometimes doesn't play well with old infrastructure.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Air travelers in the U.S. were in for a nasty surprise last Friday night after the Federal Aviation Authority (FAA) called for a nationwide ground stop. According to the Wall Street Journal, the issue started after a newly installed Juniper router, built by HP Enterprise, started broadcasting to a wide part of the network. This, in turn, overloaded the established and antiquated system, resulting in communications glitches and interruptions between air traffic control and pilots.

Thankfully, this did not affect other safety services, like Traffic Collision Avoidance System (TCAS), which primarily relied on hardware in aircraft that communicated with other transponders to help prevent accidents. Still, air traffic’s inability to communicate with high-flying aircraft made it impossible to coordinate the thousands of airborne aircraft at any given time, forcing the agency to call for an emergency ground stop. A nationwide ground stop order is quite a drastic move, and it has only been invoked twice before. The first such event was on September 11, 2001, when terrorists infamously used hijacked airliners as weapons. The second was on January 11, 2023, when the agency’s Notice to Air Missions (NOTAM) system, which sends warnings to pilots and air crews of important information crucial to flight safety, suffered from an outage for about a couple of hours.

The problem first became apparent at Indianapolis Center, which monitors the airspace in northern Kentucky, southern Indiana, southern Ohio, and parts of West Virginia, when the personnel working at the site had trouble communicating with flight crews. The agency then went on a nationwide hunt for the glitch, and it soon found two possible sources — an FAA radio site in El Dorado, Arkansas and a radar site in Amarillo, Texas. Upon deeper inspection, the agency discovered that new routers recently installed in both places have begun sending out a high volume of data across the entire FAA communications network.

“We saw two places that were actually pinging our network at a very high rate, a thousand connections over every four to five seconds,” FAA technology chief Rebecca Guy said in a press conference. This might seem like a minor hiccup for any modern network, which could typically handle connections in the hundreds of thousands, or even millions, per second. For example, one of the largest recorded DDoS attacks for 2026 saw traffic spike to 31.4 TB/s, which is enough to takedown an entire country’s web infrastructure.

However, the FAA still uses an antiquated copper network, which was easily overwhelmed by the amount of data that the routers sent. This network is already several decades old, so the agency is currently in the process of upgrading everything to fiber, which is costing taxpayers billions of dollars. Even as the agency is in this period of transition, FAA Administrator Bryan Bedford has raised several questions. “Was it a faulty installation? Was it a faulty configuration? Or was it just a bad piece of router technology that we put into this system that propagated these errors?” Bedford said in statement on Saturday. He also added that the agency is investigating the incident. As for the Juniper router, an HPE spokesperson said that they appear to be working as intended and that “They did not fail.” It was eventually discovered that a specific feature on the router caused the system overload, with FAA employees turning it off across the entire network on all Juniper routers.

It took about four hours for the FAA to return its communications network to normal, but repercussions go way beyond that. Passengers aboard all those canceled and diverted flights needed to be served, even as new passengers kept on arriving for their scheduled flights. It’s been estimated that airlines needed a few days to recover after the CrowdStrike crash of 2024 forced United, Delta, and American Airlines to cancel flights (except for Southwest, which avoided the chaos because it used an antiquated OS from the 1990s), so we expect passengers unlucky enough to be in transit during this incident to suffer waits between a few hours to a day or two before their journeys could continue.

Nvidia Board Director Mark Stevens sold about 4.2 million of his shares, netting him around $1 billion in cash, almost exactly two months before Jensen Huang was accused of having “close ties” to an alleged Chinese spy. According to Moneywise.com, Stevens started the sale from August 31 to September 2, when Nvidia stock hovered between $220 and $226, which made him $411 million. The next tranche, totaling $236 million, came in the next couple of days as Nvidia traded from $227 to $234. The final sale happened on September 18 at around $220 per share, making Stevens approximately $300 million. These three transactions would have made him somewhere around $947 million.

Last year, Jensen Huang sold up to six million Nvidia shares, netting him around $925 million. However, unlike Stevens, who had no plan, this move was announced six months earlier in a Rule 10b5-1 trading plan to help avoid accusations of insider trading.

Nvidia’s stock price has taken a beating since the scandal broke, with the company trading at $162.85 at the time of writing, its lowest level in the past 12 months. When the story first broke at the end of October, Nvidia shares were at around $205. It then started sliding, with the price at $178.67 after Jensen admitted to the affair last Sunday, although he did not name the involved party.

A second affair was revealed a few days after the former Nvidia chief’s confession, leading to his resignation from the company a few hours after that. The news sent Nvidia stock down to $151.09 — a nearly 25% drop from before the scandal. It recovered somewhat after the initial shock, climbing to nearly $170 before Friday’s trading, but took a beating again soon after the market opened.

Despite the sale, Stevens still holds about 27.5 million Nvidia shares, making him the second-largest shareholder after Jensen and Lori Huang. The price difference between today and September 18 is around $57, meaning he would have lost 25% of the value if he had sold after the scandal broke. Furthermore, his remaining Nvidia shares have lost more than $1.5 billion in value in just one quarter. Still, selling one’s holdings in a company as a board director does not constitute a crime. After all, it's unlikely that one could have predicted how hard Jensen Huang would fall in such a short time.

#10356. Pentagon tests pack hunting with autonomous war robots

But a human operator ‘has to approve any type of engagement.’

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

The Pentagon has been testing packs of autonomous ground vehicles (UGVs) in simulated combat situations. In mid-October, at Fort Knox’s St. Vith Range in Kentucky, four UGVs “worked as a team, negotiating and surveilling the rolling landscape in search of enemy forces,” recalls a DARPA blog post. These UGVs are highly modular and can be kitted out with a wide range of tactical add-ons, but target engagement was controlled by a human operator. This latest exercise was prompted by the request of European allies.

“Out here at Fort Knox, we’re looking at how autonomous mobility technology can be paired with payloads to enable new warfighter capabilities,” explained Jason Gregory, technical advisor at DARPA. The blog notes that this location was also chosen as it represents a similar landscape to the European theater.

During the demo an operator received a tip about a simulated threat alongside some satellite imagery. They then sent the pack of UGVs to the area to surveil the landscape and identify targets. Smartly, a sole UGV went ahead of the pack to scout, with the three others waiting behind. The scout UGV was equipped with a flying drone. In a real-world scenario this could have been flown for better visuals or to strike the targets from the sky. “Soldiers can mount anything and everything onto – 240 Bravos, drones, rockets,” said a technician attending the demo, as they are “super modular platforms.” The blog says three of the four UGVs in the exercise had drones.

After the designated area was scouted by the leading autonomous ground vehicle, other UGVs followed on “to provide cover and support.” A simulated rocket was fired at a target identified about 300 yards from the robot pack. Finally, the last robot came out of cover, higher up over a ridge, to assess the field of conflict.

It was emphasized that during the exercise, a human operator was controlling any kinetic engagement. “All the mobility, all the movement, all the detections, all of that is autonomous,” explained Gregory. “But they don’t pull the trigger until a human says pull the trigger.”

This exercise was part of DARPA’s Robotic Autonomy in Complex Environments with Resiliency, or RACER program. Expertise required for RACER’s success includes vehicle design, algorithm technologies, super intelligence, and secure communications.

After five years of work, these UGVs can now be instructed to complete tasks on the battlefield, instead of being manually driven, notes Gregory. They roam off-road as fast as any human driver could manage, at a minimum.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

Testers reckon the UGV packs are ready for deployment and use in “a host of other missions, including food, ammunition and supply logistics and more,” according to the DARPA blog. More UGV pack hunting tests are scheduled for December, with a group of serving soldiers involved.

#10355. How to Migrate an Elementor Site to the WordPress Block Editor

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Elementor made your site easy to build. It may also be making it slow. Here's how to move a site onto the Block Editor without breaking URLs, losing content or dropping out of Google.

Page builders solved a real problem. Before the Block Editor matured, they were the easiest way to give clients drag-and-drop layouts. Many of those sites are still running today, and a lot of them are slow on phones.

The data backs that up. According to the HTTP Archive Core Web Vitals Technology Report for June 2026, 36.8% of Elementor sites pass Core Web Vitals on mobile. Sites built with the Block Editor pass 53.0% of the time. That's correlation, not proof, since hosting and setup matter too. But in my experience, it matches what happens when you remove the builder from a slow site.

My reason for migrating sites off Elementor is simple: **page builders make websites heavy. **Having said that, Elementor has improved a lot. Its performance settings, Flexbox Containers, and asset-loading options cut much of the bloat, and a carefully tuned Elementor site can pass Core Web Vitals. But tuning only goes so far. You’re still shipping the builder’s framework, wrapper markup and scripts, and every plugin or Elementor update is a chance for the speed to slip again.

For small business sites that don’t need drag-and-drop editing, rebuilding the same design on the WordPress Block Editor (Gutenberg) removes that weight at the source, without changing what the client sees.

This guide follows the order I use. It assumes you're comfortable with WordPress admin, WP-CLI and a staging site. If you need to set those up first, SitePoint's guide to the ultimate WordPress development environment covers the tooling.

Not every Elementor site needs to move. Migration makes sense when:

It's harder to justify when the site leans on Elementor Pro features you'd need to replace, such as complex popups, dynamic loops or WooCommerce templates. Those can all be rebuilt, but the job gets bigger fast.

You can't prove a migration worked without "before" numbers. Capture these on the live site first.

Crawl the site with a tool such as Screaming Frog, and export every indexable URL with its title, meta description, H1 and canonical tag. You can also pull published pages and posts with WP-CLI:

Run Google PageSpeed Insights, which uses Lighthouse for its lab scores, on the homepage and two or three key templates. Save the mobile lab scores and, if the site has enough traffic, the field data from real users.

Export the last three months of pages and queries from Google Search Console. That's what you'll compare against after launch.

Elementor stores each page's layout as JSON in the elementordata post meta field, and marks builder pages with elementoredit_mode. This command lists every page that uses the builder:

Then list everything else Elementor controls, which is easy to miss:

On the small business sites I've migrated, the inventory usually turns up more than the owner expects. The pages are the easy part. The surprises are saved templates nobody remembers creating, a popup still running on every page, add-on widgets from plugins that were installed once and forgotten, and contact forms that live inside Elementor Pro rather than a form plugin. Finding those before the rebuild saves a broken launch.

Work on a staging copy. Install a block theme, such as Twenty Twenty-Five or a lightweight starter theme, and move the Elementor global styles into theme.json. That way colors, fonts and spacing are defined once, instead of in every widget.

Copy the hex values and font names from Elementor's Site Settings so the new site matches the old one. Rebuild the header and footer as template parts in the Site Editor. If you'd rather write a theme yourself, SitePoint's series on building a WordPress theme from scratch covers the basics.

Most Elementor sites repeat the same few sections: a hero, a services grid, testimonials and a call to action. Build each one once as a block pattern, then reuse it on every page. Patterns are plain block markup, so you don't need React to write them, whatever you may have heard about React and WordPress development.

In a block theme, any PHP file in the /patterns folder with a header comment is registered automatically:

For page content, there's a useful shortcut. Elementor saves a plain HTML copy of each page into post_content. Open a page in the Block Editor, and the old content usually appears in a Classic block. Choose Convert to blocks, then delete the leftovers and rebuild the layout with your patterns. It keeps the copy and images, so you only redo the design.

The difference shows up in the page source. Here's typical front-end markup for a single heading on an Elementor page, using the older section and column layout:

Newer Elementor versions with Flexbox containers and the optimized markup settings trim some of those wrappers, but most older sites still carry all of them. Multiply that by every element on every page, and the weight adds up.

To speed up this step, I built a free Elementor to Gutenberg converter. Paste in a page's elementordata JSON from Step 2, and it returns block markup you can paste into the Block Editor's code editor. It handles headings, text, images, buttons, spacers, dividers and column layouts, and lists anything it can't convert, such as forms and popups, for you to rebuild by hand. It runs entirely in your browser, so the page data never leaves your computer.

The code is rarely what holds a migration up. Content is. Once clients see their site being rebuilt, many decide it's time to rewrite their pages too, and the project ends up waiting on copywriters and approvals. My fix is to separate the two: migrate the existing content as it is, launch, then update the copy afterwards. That keeps the speed and SEO gains on schedule, and it means any ranking change can be traced to either the migration or the new copy, not both at once.

This is the step that decides whether rankings hold.

To spot-check a redirect or a status code from the command line:

When staging matches the live site, deploy it. Then:

Leave the old elementordata meta in place for a few weeks, in case you need to roll back. Once you're confident, take a backup and remove it to shrink the database.

Run PageSpeed Insights again straight away for lab scores. Field data takes longer: the Chrome UX Report uses a rolling 28-day window, so give it a month before judging real-user results. Compare Search Console clicks for the same period before and after launch.

In my migrations, the biggest change is usually page weight and loading time, because the builder's CSS, JavaScript and wrapper markup are gone. What doesn't always improve is server response time. If the site is on slow shared hosting, the first byte still arrives late, and that needs a hosting fix, not a rebuild. Search traffic should hold steady if the URLs and metadata were protected. If it drops, the Step 5 crawl comparison is the first place to look.

Moving off Elementor isn't a one-click job, but it isn't a full redesign either. Record a baseline, inventory what the builder controls, rebuild with patterns, and protect every URL. Most of the speed gain comes from removing code the site never needed. For what to tackle after that, see these practical web performance techniques. Most of the risk comes from rushing the SEO checks. Done carefully, the client gets the same site, a faster one, and an editor that comes with WordPress itself.

***Author Bio: ***Fahad Raza is a WordPress developer and SEO consultant based in Sydney. He has built websites since 2005, worked in SEO for more than 18 years, and spent eight and a half years on the IKEA Australia and New Zealand website.
